Directed Homotopy in Non-Positively Curved Spaces
Éric Goubault, Samuel Mimram

Abstract
. A semantics of concurrent programs can be given using precubical sets, in order to study (higher) commutations between the actions, thus encoding the “geometry” of the space of possible executions of the program. Here, we study the particular case of programs using only mutexes, which are the most widely used synchronization primitive. We show that in this case, the resulting programs have non-positive curvature , a notion that we introduce and study here for precubical sets, and can be thought of as an algebraic analogue of the well-known one for metric spaces. Using this it, as well as categorical rewriting techniques, we are then able to show that directed and non-directed homotopy coincide for directed paths in these precubical sets. Finally, we study the geometric realization of precubical sets in metric spaces, to show that our conditions on precubical sets actually coincide with those for metric spaces. Since the category of metric spaces is not cocomplete, we are lead to work with generalized metric spaces and study some of their properties.

†
†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 16, Issue 3, Paper 4

Deep and fruitful links have been unraveled over the recent years between
concurrent programs and topological spaces. The starting point of those was the definition of the
so-called “geometric semantics”, which to a concurrent program associates a
space whose points correspond to states of the program, paths to executions, and
homotopies between paths to equivalence of execution traces up to permutation of
independent actions. To be precise, in order to take in account the orientation of time,
which makes the execution of actions irreversible, one has to actually consider
spaces equipped with a notion of direction, which specifies directed paths and directed
homotopies. This point of view has lead to both theoretical and practical
applications, which are based on the fact that the study of the geometrical
features of those spaces brings us information about the possible executions of
the program, without having to consider all the possible interleavings of
actions of the various threads constituting the program. We refer to reader
to
[
goubault2000geometry
]
, as well as the recent book about the
subject
[
datc
]
, for more details.

All these techniques have thus been developed with the aim of using
mathematical tools for computing geometric invariants in order to ease the
verification of concurrent programs. However, because of the aforementioned
presence of time direction, most of those tools (such as homology groups,
homotopy groups, etc.) cannot directly be used in order to compute
the information which is relevant from a computer
scientific point of view. In this article, we focus on a particular
restricted class of programs: concurrent programs using only binary mutexes as
a synchronization primitive. While being constrained enough to enjoy interesting
properties, this class is still realistic from a practical point of view and can
be used to express many of the classical synchronization algorithms. One of the
main results of this article is that, in the geometrical models associated to
those programs,
homotopy coincides with directed homotopy
, thus allowing one to
use all tools from ordinary homotopy theory
to characterize these execution models.

The cube property.

Apart from the general motivations stated above, the origins of this article lie
in the observation that a similar property, that we call here the
cube
property
, occurs in different forms in different contexts, and it turns out
that there are good reasons for this to be the case. This property is namely

(1)

one of the main properties satisfied by semantics in asynchronous
transition systems (or precubical sets) of our class of programs, which
roughly says that when the transition system contains transitions forming half
of a cube then it also contains the other half:

(2)

the so-called Yang-Baxter axiom satisfied by symmetries in monoidal
categories, which can be depicted using string diagrams as

\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{yb_nl_l.pdf}}}\qquad\quad=\qquad\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{yb_nl_r.pdf}}}

(3)

the Gromov condition
[
gromov1987hyperbolic
]
which characterizes
cubical complexes of non-positive curvature (also called
npc
or CAT(0)
spaces).

The correspondence between the first two will be one of the main technical tools
used in order to show the coincidence between homotopy and its directed variant
mentioned above. The correspondence with the third point, reveals the main
characteristics of the geometry of the spaces obtained as semantics of programs:
if we realize them as metric spaces,
they are non-positively curved
. This
means that if we draw a triangle in such a space, whose sides are geodesics, it
will appear to be thinner than usual. This is typically the case for hyperbolic
spaces (on the left) where a triangle (also pictured in the middle) is typically
thinner than an usual triangle in a flat space (on the right):

Related work.

Since the topics covered in the various sections of the article are quite
distinct (but of course closely related), we have mentioned in each section the
related work specific to it, and only briefly expose here transversal
references.
In particular, the cube property that we use for defining
npc
precubical sets
and characterizing precubical semantics of concurrent programs and with binary
mutexes has already been used in various works, as we now briefly recall.
On the one hand this condition characterizes domains arising from event
structures, which were introduced by Winskel
[
winskel1980events
,
winskel1993models
]
. In particular, an equivalence of categories between
deterministic labeled event structures and generalized trace languages (which
satisfy three cube properties!) is given by Sassone, Nielsen and Winskel
in
[
sassone1993deterministic
]
.
This line of work is quite relevant here because event structures can be thought
of as “unfolded” variants of the programs we consider: in particular, our use
of binary mutexes corresponds to the binary conflict relation in event
structures.
However, the uses of cube conditions in order to model
concurrent processes dates back to Stark’s concurrent transition
systems
[
stark1989concurrent
]
, to the work of Panangaden and
Shanbhogue
[
panangaden1990stability
]
(where it is linked with a stability
property in domains), and to Droste
[
droste1994kleene
]
and
Kuske
[
kuske1994nondeterministic
]
who showed that concurrency automata with
two cube axioms correspond to dI-domains.
On the other hand, this condition was used by Gromov to characterize
npc
cubical
complexes
[
gromov1987hyperbolic
]
as mentioned above. The link between the
two was first explicitly made by Chepoi
[
chepoi2012nice
]
(who noticed that
event structures are in bijection with median
graphs
[
barthelemy1993median
]
, which in turn are in bijection with CAT(0)
cube complexes
[
roller1998poc
,
chepoi2000graphs
]
) and was recently
rediscovered
[
ardila2012geodesics
]
(reinventing the notion of event
structure under the prosaic name “poset with inconsistent pairs”). These
relationships can be summarized in the following diagram, where the dashed arrows
schematize some of our contributions.

npc

cube

complexes

[
chepoi2012nice
,
ardila2012geodesics
]

[
roller1998poc
,
chepoi2000graphs
]

[
gromov1987hyperbolic
]

median

graphs

[
barthelemy1993median
]

concurrent

programs

with

mutexes

cube

property

event

structures

[
nielsen1981petri
]

[
nielsen1994relationships
,
sassone1996models
]

dI domains

[
panangaden1990stability
,
kuske1994nondeterministic
]

Other uses of the cube property can be found in Lévy
[
levy1978reductions
]
(this
seems to be the first occurrence of the condition) and
Melliès’
[
gonthier1992abstract
,
mellies2002axiomatic
]
study of
standardization in rewriting systems, see also
[
bezem2003term
]
, and
Dehornoy’s work on Garside monoids
[
dehornoy2000completeness
,
dehornoy2013foundations
]
where it is used in order to ensure that a presented
category embeds in its enveloping groupoid (this is generalized to localizations
in
[
clerc2015presenting
]
), which is closely related to the situation
studied in Section
2
.

Contents of the article.

We begin by recalling the notion of precubical set (Section
1.1
), associate to
each program a precubical set (Section
1.2
) describing its execution traces
as well as (higher) commutations between their actions, and characterize their
main properties (Section
1.3
): they are geometric (Section
1.3.1
),
non-positively curved and satisfy in particular the cube condition
(Section
1.3.2
), which can be reformulated by a condition on links
(Section
1.3.3
). We then introduce the notions of homotopy and dihomotopy
between paths in precubical sets (Section
2.1
) and study those in a
2-categorical context (Section
2.2.1
), which leads us to
Theorem
39
which shows that homotopy and dihomotopy
relations coincide (Section
2.2.2
). We then recall and study in detail
generalized metric spaces (Section
3.1
), allowing us to realize a
precubical set as a metric space (Section
) which is shown in
Theorem
to be non-positively curved when obtained as
the semantics of a concurrent program (Section
).

We would like to particularly thank Emmanuel Haucourt and Jean Goubault-Larrecq
for many comments and discussions about the ideas developed in this article, as
well as the anonymous referees for helpful suggestions.

1.
Precubical semantics of concurrent programs

1.1.
Precubical sets

We first recall the definition of precubical sets which will serve as the
primary algebraic structure for defining semantics for concurrent
programs. Those can be thought of as a generalization of the notion of
graph, which incorporates the information of commutation between two actions, and
more generally between
n
actions, making them quite natural to model
concurrent programs in the spirit of “true concurrency”.

{defi}

The
precubical category

\square
is the free monoidal category containing
one object
1
, and two morphisms
\varepsilon^{-},\varepsilon^{+}:0\to 1
,
where
0
denotes the unit of the monoidal category. It can also be presented
as the category whose objects are integers and morphisms are generated by
morphisms
\varepsilon^{\epsilon}_{i,n}:n\to n+1
with
\epsilon\in\left\{-,+\right\}
,

n\in\mathbb{N}
and
0\leqslant i\leqslant n
, subject to the relations

\varepsilon^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{i,n+1}\varepsilon^{\epsilon}_{j,n}\quad=\quad\varepsilon^{\epsilon}_{j+1,n+1}\varepsilon^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{i,n}

(1)

whenever
i\leqslant j
and
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in\left\{-,+\right\}
. The category of

precubical sets
is the category
\hat{\square}
of presheaves over this
category.

A precubical set
C\in\hat{\square}
thus consists of a family of sets

(C(n))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
together with
maps
\partial^{\epsilon}_{i,n}:C(n+1)\to C(n)
, with
0\leqslant i\leqslant n
and

\epsilon\in\left\{-,+\right\}
, where the map
\partial^{\epsilon}_{i,n}
is a notation
for
C(\varepsilon^{\epsilon}_{i,n})
, satisfying relations which are dual to
(
1
), and we sometimes simply write
\partial^{\epsilon}_{i}
when
n

is clear from the context. An element
c\in C(n)
is called an
n
-cube of

C
,
\partial^{\epsilon}_{i,n}(c)
is called a
face
of
c
, and given a
morphism
\phi:n\to p
in
\square
,
C(\phi)(c)
is called an
iterated face

of
c
. The
dimension
of a precubical set
C
is the smallest integer

d\in\mathbb{N}\uplus\left\{\infty\right\}
such that
C(n)=\emptyset
for
n>d
. A precubical
set
C
is
finite
when it is finite-dimensional and each
C(n)
is
finite.

{exa}

We write
\vec{I}
for the precubical set of dimension 1, called the

standard interval
,

\textstyle{x\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{y}

with
\vec{I}(0)=\left\{x,y\right\}
,
\vec{I}(1)=\left\{a\right\}
,
\vec{I}(n)=\emptyset
for

n\geqslant 2
,
\partial_{0}^{-}(a)=x
,
\partial_{0}^{+}(a)=y
.

{exa}

A simple example of a precubical set
C
of dimension 2 is

\textstyle{x\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{b}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b^{\prime}}
\textstyle{x\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{y}

with
C(0)=\left\{x,y\right\}
,
C(1)=\left\{a,b,b^{\prime}\right\}
and
C(2)=\left\{\alpha\right\}
. The cells
of dimension 0 are pictured as points, cells of dimension 1 as segments, and
the cell of dimension 2 as a square.
This precubical set can thus abstractly be thought of as a cylinder. Face maps
are given by
\partial^{-}_{0}(a)=x
,
\partial^{+}_{0}(a)=y
,

\partial^{-}_{0}(\alpha)=b
,
\partial^{+}_{0}(\alpha)=b^{\prime}
,

\partial^{-}_{1}(\alpha)=\partial^{+}_{1}(\alpha)=a
, etc.

We recall some operations available on precubical sets, which will be used in
the following in order to define the semantics for programs.
A tensor product can be defined as follows.

{defi}

Given two precubical sets
C
and
D
, their
tensor product

C\otimes D
is the precubical set defined by

(C\otimes D)(n)\quad=\quad\coprod_{i+j=n}C(i)\times D(j)

for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and

\partial^{\epsilon}_{k,n}(C\otimes D)(c,d)\quad=\quad\begin{cases}(\partial^{\epsilon}_{k,n}(c),d)&\text{if $0\leqslant k<i$}\\
(c,\partial^{\epsilon}_{k-i,n}(d))&\text{if $i\leqslant k<n$}\end{cases}

for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\leqslant k<n
,
\epsilon\in\left\{0,1\right\}
and
(c,d)\in C(i)\times D(j)\subseteq(C\otimes D)(n)
with
i+j=n
.
This tensor equips the category
\hat{\square}
with a monoidal structure, whose
unit is the precubical set
C
consisting of one single
0
-cube.

As a presheaf category, the category of precubical sets is cocomplete, with
colimits being computed pointwise
[
maclane:cwm
]
. In particular, we denote
by
C\sqcup D
the coproduct of two cubical sets, and given a precubical set
C

and two
0
-cubes
c,d\in C(0)
, we write
C[c=d]
for the precubical set
obtained from
C
by identifying
c
and
d
(this precubical set can be
obtained as the expected coequalizer). Finally, given a set
X\subseteq C(0)
of

0
-cubes of a precubical set
C
, we write
C\setminus X
for the

precubical subset
of
C
consisting of
n
-cubes of
C
which do not contain an element of
X
as
iterated face.

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we write
\square_{n}
for the full subcategory of
\square
whose
objects
k
satisfy
k\leqslant n
, and
\hat{\square}_{n}
is thus the full subcategory
of
\hat{\square}
consisting of precubical sets of dimension at most
n
.

{defi}

The presheaves in
\square_{n}
are called
n
-precubical sets
. The functor

\hat{\square}\to\hat{\square}_{n}
induced by precomposition with the inclusion functor

\square_{n}\hookrightarrow\square
is called the
n
-truncation functor
.

In particular, the category
\hat{\square}_{1}
is the category of graphs, and every
precubical set
C
has an underlying graph: we thus sometimes refer to the
elements of
C(0)
(resp.
C(1)
) as
vertices
(resp.
edges
or

transitions
), a
path
in a precubical set is a path in the
underlying directed graph, etc. Also, a 2-cube is sometimes called a

square
.

{defi}

We define a symmetric relation
\diamond
on paths of length 2 as the smallest
symmetric relation such that, given two paths
a\cdot b
and
b^{\prime}\cdot a^{\prime}
of
length 2 constituted of edges
a
,
b
,
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
, we have

a\cdot b\diamond b^{\prime}\cdot a^{\prime}
whenever there exists a square
\alpha
such that

a=\partial_{0,1}^{-}(\alpha)\qquad\qquad b=\partial_{1,1}^{+}(\alpha)\qquad\qquad b^{\prime}=\partial_{1,1}^{-}(\alpha)\qquad\qquad a^{\prime}=\partial_{0,1}^{+}(\alpha)

or symmetrically. Graphically, we have a square
\alpha
as on the left, which
we often schematically picture as on the right

\textstyle{x_{11}}
\textstyle{x_{10}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b=\partial_{1,1}^{+}(\alpha)}
\textstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces x_{01}}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{0,1}^{+}(\alpha)=a^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces x_{00}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a=\partial_{0,1}^{-}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{1,1}^{-}(\alpha)=b^{\prime}}

\textstyle{x_{11}}
\textstyle{x_{10}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{\diamond}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces x_{01}}
\scriptstyle{a^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces x_{00}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{b^{\prime}}

(2)

In this situation, we say that the coinitial transitions
a
and
b^{\prime}
(resp.
b^{\prime}
and
a
) are
independent
.

As for any presheaf category, there is a full and faithful embedding

Y:\square\to\hat{\square}
given by the Yoneda functor, which is defined on objects as
the set of morphisms
Ynm=\square(m,n)
. Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the representable
precubical set
Yn
is called the
standard
n
-cube
. It can be shown that

Yn
is isomorphic to
\vec{I}^{\otimes n}
, the tensor product of
n
copies of

\vec{I}
(see Example
1.1
). For instance, we have

An explicit description of standard
n
-cubes can be given as follows. We will
see that it is often quite useful in order to perform computations.

Lemma 1
.

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the cubes in
Yn
are in bijection with strings in

\left\{-,0,+\right\}^{n}
, the
k
-cubes in
Ynk
being the strings containing the
letter
0
exactly
k
times, and given
u\in Ynk
, the face
\partial_{i}^{-}(u)

(resp.
\partial_{i}^{+}(u)
) is obtained from
u
by replacing the
i
-th
letter
0
by
-
(resp.
+
).

For instance, the preceding standard cubes are

\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad\qquad\qquad}c@{\qquad\qquad\qquad}c@{\qquad\qquad\qquad}c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 16.29167pt\raise-14.41667pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{00}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 33.13892pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 40.5556pt\raise-14.41667pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{0+}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.5556pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-7.41669pt\raise-28.83334pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\scriptstyle+-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.21529pt\raise-34.50557pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{+0}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.41669pt\raise-28.83334pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 31.41669pt\raise-28.83334pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\scriptstyle++}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\\
Y0&Y1&Y2\end{array}

(here
\varepsilon
denotes the empty word). One can notice that
Ynk=\emptyset

for
k>n
and there is only one element in
Ynn
(the string
0^{n}
). The

standard hollow
n
-cube
, denoted
\partial Yn
, is the precubical set
obtained from
Yn
by removing the cell in
Ynn
. For instance,

\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad\qquad\qquad}c@{\qquad\qquad\qquad}c@{\qquad\qquad\qquad}c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 4.34723pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.34723pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle-}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 28.34723pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle+}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 12.68059pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.69446pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\scriptstyle--}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.93752pt\raise 5.25555pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-0}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.13892pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.68059pt\raise-14.41667pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{0-}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 33.13892pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\scriptstyle-+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.5556pt\raise-14.41667pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{0+}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.5556pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-7.41669pt\raise-28.83334pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\scriptstyle+-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.21529pt\raise-34.50557pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{+0}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.41669pt\raise-28.83334pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 31.41669pt\raise-28.83334pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\scriptstyle++}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\\
\partial Y0&\partial Y1&\partial Y2\end{array}

There is an obvious inclusion morphism of precubical sets

\partial Yn\to Yn
embedding the standard hollow
n
-cube as the “border” of
the standard
n
-cube. A fact that will sometimes be useful is
that the faces of the standard
n
-cubes naturally index morphisms computing
iterated faces of an
n
-cube: given a precubical set
C
, an
n
-cube

x\in C(n)
and
u\in\left\{-,0,+\right\}^{n}
, we write

\partial^{u}(x)\quad=\quad\partial_{0}^{u_{0}}\circ\partial_{1}^{u_{1}}\circ\ldots\circ\partial_{n-1}^{u_{n-1}}(x)

where
u_{i}\in\left\{-,0,+\right\}
is the
i
-th letter of
u
and by convention

\partial_{i}^{0}(x)=x
.

Given a set
\mathcal{L}
of
labels
, we will sometimes consider labeled
variants of precubical sets.

{defi}

A
labeled
precubical set
(C,\ell)
consists of a precubical set
C

together with a labeling function
\ell:C(1)\to\mathcal{L}
on edges, such that
we have

\ell\circ\partial^{-}_{i,1}=\ell\circ\partial^{+}_{i,1}
for
i\in\left\{0,1\right\}
.

Graphically, the condition on the labeling function amounts to supposing that
parallel edges of a square have the same label.

Remark 2
.

If we suppose that the set
\mathcal{L}
is totally ordered, we can define a
precubical set
\operatorname{!}\mathcal{L}
such that the elements of
\operatorname{!}\mathcal{L}(n)
are
strictly increasing sequences of
n
elements of
\mathcal{L}
, and given

c\in\operatorname{!}\mathcal{L}(n+1)
,
\partial^{-}_{i,n}(c)=\partial^{+}_{i,n}(c)
is obtained
by removing the
i
-th element of the sequence
c
. The category of labeled
precubical sets can then alternatively be defined as the comma category

\hat{\square}/\operatorname{!}
, see
[
goubault2012formal
]
for details.

We will also make some use of presimplicial sets, which are a well-known variant
of precubical sets, which is based on triangles instead of squares. Formally,
the category of
(augmented) presimplicial sets
is the presheaf
category
\hat{\bigtriangleup}
, where
\bigtriangleup
is the category whose objects are finite
ordinals
[n]
(given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we write
[n]
for the set

\left\{0,\ldots,n-1\right\}
) and morphisms are strictly increasing functions. Given a
presimplicial set
S
, the elements of
S(n)
are called

n
-simplices
, and, given
i
with
0\leqslant i\leqslant n
, we write

\partial_{i,n}:S(n+1)\to S(n)
for the image by
S
of the function

[n]\to[n+1]
, whose image does not contain
i
, associating to an

(n+1)
-simplex its
i
-th face.

1.2.
Precubical semantics of concurrent programs

We now introduce an idealized concurrent programming language and provide its
semantics in precubical sets. A detailed presentation of this language can be
found in
[
datc
]
, in a variant which includes more realistic features, such
as memory and manipulation of values.

We suppose fixed a set
\mathcal{A}
of
actions
, containing a particular
action
\mathsf{nop}\in\mathcal{A}
(standing for the action which has no effect), and a
set
\mathcal{M}
of
mutexes
. A
program

p
is an expression
generated by the grammar

p\quad::=\quad\mathsf{1}\quad|\quad A\quad|\quad\mathsf{P}_{a}\quad|\quad\mathsf{V}_{a}\quad|\quad p\operatorname{.}p\quad|\quad p+p\quad|\quad p\operatorname{\|}p\quad|\quad p^{*}

where
A\in\mathcal{A}
is an arbitrary action and
a\in\mathcal{M}
is an arbitrary
mutex. The intended meaning of these constructions is the following one:
\mathsf{1}

is the empty program,
A\in\mathcal{A}
is an arbitrary instruction (for instance,
assigning a value to a memory cell or printing a message on the screen),

p\operatorname{.}q
is
p
followed by
q
,
p+q
is a (non-deterministic) choice
between
p
and
q
,
p\operatorname{\|}q
is the program obtained by running
p
and
q

in parallel,
p^{*}
is the program
p
executed an arbitrary number of
times. In practice, it is desirable to forbid some actions (such as accessing
memory) from running in parallel. This is generally done using mutexes which are
particular kind of resources on which a program can perform two operations: a
mutex
a
can either be
locked
using the instruction
\mathsf{P}_{a}
or

released
using the instruction
\mathsf{V}_{a}
. The operational semantics of
a mutex is such that at most one subprogram can have locked a mutex at a time:
if a subprogram tries to lock an already-locked mutex, it is frozen until the
mutex is released.

The class of programs defined above is very general and thus difficult to reason
about. In practice, the programs which are written are such that the state of
mutexes only depends on the position in the program and not on the execution
that lead to it. We will thus restrict to such programs, called

conservative
, which can be formally defined as follows.

{defi}

The
resource consumption
function
\Delta(p):\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{Z}
of a
program
p
is defined inductively by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta(\mathsf{1})&\quad=\quad 0&&&\Delta(A)&\quad=\quad 0\\
\Delta(\mathsf{P}_{a})&\quad=\quad-\delta_{a}&&&\Delta(\mathsf{V}_{a})&\quad=\quad\delta_{a}\\
\Delta(p\operatorname{.}q)&\quad=\quad\Delta(p)+\Delta(q)&&&\Delta(p\operatorname{\|}q)&\quad=\quad\Delta(p)+\Delta(q)\\
\Delta(p+q)&\quad=\quad\Delta(p)&&\text{if $\Delta(p)=\Delta(q)$}\\
\Delta(p^{*})&\quad=\quad 0&&\text{if $\Delta(p)=0$}\end{aligned}

Above,
0
denotes the constant function, the sum of functions is computed
pointwise, and
\delta_{a}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{Z}
is the function such that

\delta_{a}(a)=1
and
\delta_{a}(b)=0
for
b\neq a
. Note that, because of the
side conditions in the cases for choice and loop, this function might not be
defined. A program
p
such that
\Delta(p)
is defined is called

conservative
.

{exa}

The program
\mathsf{P}_{a}\operatorname{.}\left(\mathsf{V}_{a}\operatorname{.}\mathsf{P}_{a}\right)^{*}\operatorname{.}\mathsf{V}_{a}
is conservative,
as well as
\mathsf{P}_{a}\operatorname{\|}\mathsf{P}_{b}
, but not the program
\mathsf{P}_{a}^{*}
(the number of
times
a
is taken depends on the number of loops executed) nor
\mathsf{P}_{a}+\mathsf{P}_{b}
(which
mutex is taken in the end depends on the chosen branch).

In the following, we will consider the following set of labels:

\mathcal{L}\quad=\quad\mathcal{A}\uplus\left\{\mathsf{P}_{a}\ \middle|\ a\in\mathcal{M}\right\}\uplus\left\{\mathsf{V}_{a}\ \middle|\ a\in\mathcal{M}\right\}

To any program
p
can be inductively associated a precubical set
C_{p}

labeled in
\mathcal{L}
, together with two vertices
b_{p}
and
e_{p}
, as
follows:

•

empty
:
C_{\mathsf{1}}
is the precubical set reduced to one vertex (and

b_{\mathsf{1}}
and
e_{\mathsf{1}}
are both equal to this vertex):

C_{\mathsf{1}}\quad=\quad b_{\mathsf{1}}=e_{\mathsf{1}}

•

action
:
C_{A}
is the graph

C_{A}\quad=\quad\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 8.40279pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.40279pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{b_{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.48697pt\raise 5.39168pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.39168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{A}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.40279pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 32.40279pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{e_{A}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}

•

lock
:
C_{\mathsf{P}_{a}}
is the graph

C_{\mathsf{P}_{a}}\quad=\quad\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 9.81801pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.81801pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{b_{\mathsf{P}_{a}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.48698pt\raise 5.93056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.93056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{P}_{a}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.81801pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 33.81801pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{e_{\mathsf{P}_{a}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}

•

release
:
C_{\mathsf{V}_{a}}
is the graph

C_{\mathsf{V}_{a}}\quad=\quad\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 9.91524pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.91524pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{b_{\mathsf{V}_{a}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.48698pt\raise 5.93056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.93056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{V}_{a}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.91524pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 33.91524pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{e_{\mathsf{V}_{a}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}

•

sequence
:

C_{p\operatorname{.}q}\quad=\quad(C_{p}\sqcup C_{q})[e_{p}=b_{q}]

with
b_{p\operatorname{.}q}=b_{p}
and
e_{p\operatorname{.}q}=e_{q}
,

•

choice
:

C_{p+q}\quad=\quad(C_{p^{\prime}}\sqcup C_{q^{\prime}})[b_{p^{\prime}}=b_{q^{\prime}}][e_{p^{\prime}}=e_{q^{\prime}}]

with
b_{p+q}=b_{p^{\prime}}=b_{q^{\prime}}
and
e_{p+q}=e_{p^{\prime}}=e_{q^{\prime}}
, where
p^{\prime}=\mathsf{nop}\operatorname{.}p
and
q^{\prime}=\mathsf{nop}\operatorname{.}q
,

•

parallel
:

C_{p\operatorname{\|}q}\quad=\quad C_{p}\otimes C_{q}

with
b_{p\operatorname{\|}q}=(b_{p},b_{q})
and
e_{p\operatorname{\|}q}=(e_{p},e_{q})
,

•

loop
:

C_{p^{*}}\quad=\quad(C_{p^{\prime}}\sqcup C_{\mathsf{nop}})[b_{p^{\prime}}=e_{p^{\prime}}][b_{p^{\prime}}=b_{\mathsf{nop}}]

with
b_{p^{*}}=b_{p^{\prime}}
and
e_{p^{*}}=e_{\mathsf{nop}}
, where

p^{\prime}=\mathsf{nop}\operatorname{.}p
.

The last cases can be illustrated as follows:

{exa}

The precubical sets associated to the program
(\mathsf{P}_{a}\operatorname{\|}\mathsf{P}_{b})+\mathsf{P}_{c}
and to
the program
\left(\mathsf{P}_{a}\operatorname{.}\mathsf{V}_{a}\right)\operatorname{\|}\left(\mathsf{P}_{a}\operatorname{.}\mathsf{V}_{a}\right)
are respectively

\textstyle{x\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{nop}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{nop}}
\textstyle{y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{P}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{P}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\diamond}
\textstyle{y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{P}_{b}}
\textstyle{z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{P}_{c}}
\textstyle{y^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{P}_{b}}
\textstyle{z^{\prime}}

\scriptstyle{\mathsf{P}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{P}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\diamond}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{P}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{V}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\diamond}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{P}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{V}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{P}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\diamond}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{V}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{V}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\diamond}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{V}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{P}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{V}_{a}}

Remark 3
.

The transitions labeled by
\mathsf{nop}
abstractly represent the evaluation of
conditions (depending on which the branch of a conditional branching is chosen
or a loop is stopped). If our language contained boolean expressions, they
should actually be such, see
[
datc
]
. Notice that these transitions cannot
be contracted to a point in the semantics because it not be correct anymore:
for instance the semantics of
\mathsf{P}_{a}+\mathsf{1}
would contain a loop.

A path
t
of length
n
in
C_{p}
, where the labels of its vertices are

l_{1},l_{2},\ldots,l_{n}\in\mathcal{L}
, can be seen as a program

l_{1}\operatorname{.}l_{2}\ldots l_{n}
(i.e. a sequence of actions and operations on mutexes),
and we write
\Delta(t):\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{Z}
for its resource consumption. When the
program is conservative, resource consumption only depends on the endpoints of
paths, which explains the terminology, by analogy with the situation in physics:

Lemma 4
.

Given a conservative program
p
and two paths
t
and
u
in
C_{p}
with
the same source and the same target, we have
\Delta(t)=\Delta(u)
.

It can be observed that, for any program
p
, there is a path in
C_{p}
from

b_{p}
to any vertex
x
. This fact and preceding lemma ensure that the
following definition makes sense:

{defi}

The
resource potential

r_{p}:C_{p}(0)\to(\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{Z})
of a
conservative program
p
is the function which to a vertex
x\in C_{p}(0)

associates
\Delta(t)
where
t
is any path from
b_{p}
to
x
.

A vertex
x
such that there exists
a\in\mathcal{M}
where

r_{p}(x)<-1
or
r_{p}(x)>0
is said to be

forbidden
: the operational semantics of mutexes enforces that such a
state of the program can never be reached, because a mutex can be taken at most
once and not released more than taken. The precubical semantics of a
conservative program
p
is thus obtained from
C_{p}
by removing those
forbidden vertices. Given a precubical set
C
and a set
X\subseteq C(0)
of
vertices of
C
, recall that we write
C\setminus X
for the precubical set
obtained from
C
by removing all the cubes having an element of
X
as iterated
face.

{defi}

Given a conservative program
p
, its
precubical semantics
is the
precubical set
\check{C}_{p}
defined by

\check{C}_{p}\quad=\quad C_{p}\setminus X\qquad\text{where}\qquad X\quad=\quad\left\{x\in C(0)\ \middle|\ r_{p}(x)<-1\lor r_{p}(x)>0\right\}

{exa}

The precubical semantics of the program

\left(\mathsf{P}_{a}\operatorname{.}\mathsf{V}_{a}\right)\operatorname{\|}\left(\mathsf{P}_{a}\operatorname{.}\mathsf{V}_{a}\right)

\scriptstyle{\mathsf{P}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{P}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{V}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{P}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{V}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{V}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{P}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{V}_{a}}

is obtained by removing then central vertex (and associated transitions) from
the graph of Example
1.2
.

An
execution trace

t
of a program
p
is a path in
\check{C}_{p}
starting
from the beginning vertex
b_{p}
. It can be checked that those are in
bijection with execution traces as they would be defined in a reasonable
operational semantics
[
datc
]
. Moreover,
n
-cubes of dimension
2
and
higher can be used to construct a useful notion of equivalence of execution
traces, called homotopy, as explained in Section
2
.

1.3.
Non-positively curved precubical sets

In this section, we study the general properties of precubical sets arising as
semantics of programs. This will lead us to formulate a set of conditions
characterizing what we call
non-positively curved precubical sets
: it
will be shown in Section
that their geometric
realizations are precisely non-positively curved precubical complexes.

1.3.1.
Geometric precubical sets

We begin by recalling the definition of “geometric” precubical
sets
[
fajstrup2005dipaths
]
. The reason for this terminology should become
apparent in Proposition
: their geometric
realizations are cubical complexes.

{defi}

A precubical set
C
is
geometric
when it satisfies the following
conditions:

(1)

no self-intersection
: two distinct iterated faces of a cube in

C
are distinct, i.e. given two morphisms
\phi,\psi:m\to n
in
\square
, if there
exists
c\in C(n)
such that
C(\phi)(c)=C(\psi)(c)
then
\phi=\psi
,

(2)

maximal common faces
: two cubes admitting a common face admit a
maximal common face.

{exa}

Consider the following precubical sets of dimension 1

32.48843pt\raise-28.80554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 65.11923pt\raise-28.80554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise (1)&(2)&(3)&(4)\end{array}

(1) and (3) are not self-intersecting but fail to have maximal common faces,
(2) is self-intersecting and (4) is geometric. Notice that (4) and (3) are
obtained by subdividing (2). However, it is not always possible to subdivide
enough a precubical set in order to obtain a geometric
one
[
fajstrup2005dipaths
]
.

{exa}

Consider the precubical set obtained from two copies of the standard square

Y2
by identifying both vertices
{\scriptstyle-+}
and both vertices
{\scriptstyle+-}
in
the two squares:

\textstyle{y_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{b^{\prime}}
\textstyle{x\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{x^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{y^{\prime}}
\textstyle{y}
\textstyle{y_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{a^{\prime}}

with
a\cdot b^{\prime}\diamond a^{\prime}\cdot b
and
f\cdot g^{\prime}\diamond g\cdot f^{\prime}
. This precubical set is
not self-intersecting but does not have maximal common faces.

Interestingly, the geometricity condition can be characterized as follows on the
category of elements of a precubical set (this classical notion is recalled in
Section
):

Lemma 5
.

A precubical set
C
is geometric if and only if its category of
elements
\mathop{\mathrm{El}}(C)
is a poset such that any two elements with a common lower
bound have a greatest lower bound, i.e. for every
x\in\mathop{\mathrm{El}}(C)
the slice
category
x/\mathop{\mathrm{El}}(C)
is a meet semilattice.

Proof 1.1
.

The assumption that
C
has no self-intersection implies that any two
morphisms with the same source and the same target in
\mathop{\mathrm{El}}(C)
are equal, and
since the non-trivial morphisms are from a cube to a cube of strictly higher
dimension, there are no non-trivial endomorphisms. The category
\mathop{\mathrm{El}}(C)
is
thus a poset. The condition on lower bounds is a direct reformulation of the
“maximal common face” condition.

The precubical semantics of most programs is a geometric precubical set,
excepting for some degenerated ones. For instance, the semantics of

\mathsf{1}+\mathsf{1}
is

\scriptstyle{\mathsf{nop}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{nop}}

which is not self-intersecting. Not allowing the construction
\mathsf{1}
to occur
in programs is enough to ensure that their semantics is geometric and does not
remove any expressive power to the language in practice. We will implicitly
assume that this is the case in the rest of the paper.
By an easy induction on the structure of programs, the following can be shown:

Lemma 6
.

The precubical semantics
\check{C}_{p}
of a program
p
without occurrences
of
\mathsf{1}
is a geometric precubical set.

1.3.2.
Non-positively curved precubical sets

In this section, we introduce the notion of non-positively curved precubical
set, which will be shown to be an algebraic counterpart of the usual notion of
non-positively curved space in Section
. The conditions for being
such a precubical set are easily formulated as lifting properties, which are of
the following general form.

{defi}

Given a morphism
f:D\to E
between precubical sets, we say that a precubical
set
C

lifts at least once
(resp.
lifts at most once
, resp.
lifts
uniquely
) the morphism
f
, when for every morphism
h:D\to C
, there
exists a morphism (resp. at most one morphism, resp. a unique morphism)

g:E\to C
such that
h=g\circ f
.

\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}

We will be mainly interested in the following lifting properties, which all more
or less express the fact that, as soon as a precubical set contains a subset
which could be the border of a cube, it should actually contain a cube with this
border.

{defi}

Suppose given a precubical set
C
. We say that
C
has

(1)

no parallel edges
when it lifts at most once the canonical
inclusion
\partial Y1\hookrightarrow Y1
,

(2)

the
at most one square closing property
when each of the four
canonical inclusions of

\textstyle{\scriptstyle++}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle-+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0+}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\scriptstyle--}}
\scriptstyle{-0}

\textstyle{\scriptstyle++}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\scriptstyle+-}}
\scriptstyle{+0}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle--}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0-}

\textstyle{\scriptstyle-+}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle+-}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\scriptstyle--}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-0}
\scriptstyle{0-}

\textstyle{\scriptstyle++}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle-+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0+}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\scriptstyle+-}}
\scriptstyle{+0}

(3)

into

Y2\quad=\quad\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 7.41669pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 20.33331pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle++}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 55.94446pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-7.41669pt\raise-17.74997pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\scriptstyle-+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.80774pt\raise-3.20276pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{0+}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern
