Remark 7
.

When a precubical set satisfies the at most one square closing property, the
liftings of (
3
) and (
3
) are necessarily unique.

Remark 8
.

Notice that, in the cube property, we require lifting four inclusions of a
“half cube” into the hollow cube, but there are four more natural inclusions
that we could write (there are height in total, one corresponding to each
vertex of the cube). However, those are superfluous, because the cube has
non-trivial automorphisms. For instance, the lifting of the inclusion of

\textstyle{\scriptstyle+--}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle+-+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+-0}
\scriptstyle{+00}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle++-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+0-}
\scriptstyle{00-}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle---}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0--}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle+++}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+0+}
\scriptstyle{++0}
\scriptstyle{0+0}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle-+-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0+-}
\scriptstyle{-0-}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle-++}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0++}
\scriptstyle{-+0}

into
\partial Y3
can be deduced from the lifting of the inclusion
corresponding to the left of (
3
).

By definition of the condition of being geometric
(Definition
1.3.1
), we have the following.

Lemma 9
.

A geometric precubical set
C
lifts at most once the embedding

\partial Yn\hookrightarrow Yn
for any
n\geqslant 1
. In particular, it has no parallel
edges. Moreover,
C
satisfies the at most one square filling property.

We introduce the following terminology, whose meaning should be explained in
Section
.

{defi}

A precubical set which

(1)

is geometric,

(2)

satisfies the cube property,

(3)

and satisfies the unique
n
-cube property for
n\geqslant 3
,

is said to be
non-positively curved
(or
npc

for short).

Proposition 10
.

The precubical semantics of a program is non-positively curved.

Proof 1.2
.

We have already seen in Lemma
6
that the cubical semantics of
a non-degenerated program is geometric. We show the other properties.
Given a program
p
, it is easy to show by induction on
p
that the
precubical set
C_{p}
is non-positively curved (essentially, one has to
check that the properties (2) and (3) are preserved by gluing along vertices
and taking tensor product). The precubical semantics is defined as

\check{C}_{p}=C_{p}\setminus X
where
X
is the set of forbidden
vertices. Clearly, the unique
n
-cube property, with
n\geqslant 3
, is still
satisfied for
\check{C}_{p}
. However, the cube property requires a little more
care. Write
L
(resp.
R
) for the precubical set on the left (resp. right)
of (
3
). Notice that the hollow 3-cube
\partial Y3
can be
obtained by gluing
L
and
R
along their “border” (i.e. by identifying the
vertices and edges with the same names in both precubical sets). Suppose given
a morphism
L\to\check{C}_{p}
: we are going to show that it can be extended as a
morphism
\partial Y3\to\check{C}_{p}
. By abuse of notation, we identify
L
with
its image in
\check{C}_{p}
and simply say that
\check{C}_{p}
“contains”
L
. Since

C_{p}
satisfies the cube property, it also contains
R
. Thus, in

\check{C}_{p}
,
L
can be completed as a hollow 3-cube unless the vertex

{\scriptstyle-+-}
is forbidden. We write
A
,
B
and
C
for the actions
respectively labeling the edges of the form
0\epsilon\epsilon^{\prime}
,

\epsilon 0\epsilon^{\prime}
and
\epsilon\epsilon^{\prime}0
, with

\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in\left\{-,+\right\}
:
L
and
R
in
C_{p}
are respectively
labeled as

\textstyle{\scriptstyle+++}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle++-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{\diamond}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle+-+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{\diamond}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle-++}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle+--}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{\diamond}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle--+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{B}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle---}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{C}

\textstyle{\scriptstyle+++}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle++-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{\diamond}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle-++}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle+--}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{\diamond}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle-+-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{\diamond}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle--+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle---}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{C}

Suppose that
{\scriptstyle-+-}
is forbidden. Since none of the vertices in
L
is
forbidden, there exists a resource
a
such that either

r_{p}({\scriptstyle-+-})(a)=1
and
B
is
\mathsf{V}_{a}
, or
r_{p}({\scriptstyle-+-})=-2
and

B
is
\mathsf{P}_{a}
. Suppose that we are in the first case (the other case is
similar), i.e.
B=\mathsf{V}_{a}
. Notice that we have

r_{p}({\scriptstyle---})=0
. Necessarily, we have
A=\mathsf{P}_{a}
(otherwise

r_{p}({\scriptstyle++-})=r_{p}({\scriptstyle-+-})=1
and the vertex
{\scriptstyle++-}
would
be forbidden) and
C=\mathsf{P}_{a}
(otherwise
{\scriptstyle-++}
would be forbidden). But in
this case, we have
r_{p}({\scriptstyle+-+})=-2
and therefore
{\scriptstyle+-+}
would be
forbidden, contradicting the hypothesis that
\check{C}_{p}
contains
L
. All other
cases can be handled by similar reasoning.

Remark 11
.

The precubical semantics of the program
A+B
is

\scriptstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{nop}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{nop}}
\scriptstyle{B}

showing that the
2
-cube property is not generally satisfied for cubical
semantics of programs.

1.3.3.
The link condition

The Gromov condition for characterizing
npc
cubical complexes, which is
recalled in Section
, is generally expressed in terms of a
“flagness” condition on the “link” of vertices in the complex. We extend
here the definition of link to precubical sets and show that a similar
characterization of
npc
can be formulated in terms of those links.

{defi}

Given a precubical set
C
, the
link
of
C
, denoted
\operatorname{link}(C)
, is
the (augmented) presimplicial set whose
n
-simplices are pairs
(u,y)
with

u\in\left\{-,+\right\}^{n}
and
y\in C(n)
. Given
i
with
i\in[n]
, the
i
-th
simplicial face of such an
n
-simplex
(u,y)
is given by

\partial_{i}(u,y)\quad=\quad(u^{\prime},\partial_{i}^{u_{i}}(y))

where
u^{\prime}
is the word
u
with the
i
-th letter removed. Given a
vertex
x\in C(0)
, the link of
x
, denoted
\operatorname{link}(x)
, is the presimplicial
subset of
\operatorname{link}(C)
whose simplices are those having
(\varepsilon,x)
as
iterated
0
-face.

Note that, by definition, the link of a vertex has only one 0-simplex, and as
such can be seen as a non-augmented presimplicial set, shifting dimensions by
-1. This will implicitly be used in the following in order to draw pictures.

{exa}

Consider the precubical set
C
on the left. The link of the vertex
x
is
shown on the right:

\textstyle{z^{\prime}}
\textstyle{y_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{z}
\textstyle{y_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{x\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{b}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{y_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{a}
\textstyle{b\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{c}

The link of a vertex
x
is constituted of the cubes having
x
as iterated
face. Its simplices can be represented as follows.

{defi}

Given
u\in\left\{-,+\right\}^{n}
, we write
\Lambda^{u}
for the precubical subset of

\partial Yn
whose cubes
v\in\left\{-,0,+\right\}^{n}
are those having at least one
letter in common with
u
(i.e. there exists
i
, with
0\leqslant i<n
, such that

v_{i}=u_{i}
).

Remark 12
.

The precubical set
\Lambda^{u}
can alternatively defined from
\partial Yn
by
removing all cubes having the vertex
\overline{u}
as iterated face, where

\overline{u}=\overline{u_{0}}\ldots\overline{u_{n-1}}
with
\overline{-}=+
and
\overline{+}=-
.

{exa}

The precubical sets (
3
) are respectively
\Lambda^{-+}
,

\Lambda^{+-}
,
\Lambda^{--}
and
\Lambda^{++}
. Those of (
3
)
are respectively
\Lambda^{+-+}
and
\Lambda^{-+-}
, and similarly for
(
3
), see also Remark
13
.

Remark 13
.

By definition, given
u\in\left\{-,+\right\}^{n}
, there is a canonical inclusion

\Lambda^{u}\hookrightarrow\partial Yn
and thus a canonical inclusion
\Lambda^{u}\hookrightarrow Yn

by post-composing with the canonical inclusion
\partial Yn\hookrightarrow Yn
. By
Remark
8
, the cube property is precisely the lifting
property wrt all possible such inclusions in the case
n=3
. In particular,
the two precubical sets of (
3
) are respectively
\Lambda^{+-+}

and
\Lambda^{-+-}
, and those of (
3
) are respectively

\Lambda^{---}
and
\Lambda^{+++}
.

Geometric precubical sets which are non-positively curved can be characterized
by a lifting condition on the links. We write
Z:\bigtriangleup\to\hat{\bigtriangleup}
for the Yoneda
embedding of the presimplicial category into presimplicial sets (the notation

Y
is reserved for the Yoneda embedding
Y:\square\to\hat{\square}
). Given
n\in\bigtriangleup
,
the representable
Zn
is called the
standard
n
-simplex
. As in the
case of precubical sets, it can be explicitly described as the presimplicial set
whose
k
-simplices are the sets
\left\{j_{0},\ldots,j_{k-1}\right\}\subseteq[n]

such that
j_{0}<j_{1}<\ldots<j_{k-1}
and, given
i
with
0\leqslant i<k
the
i
-th
face of such a simplex is given by removing the element
j_{i}
. We thus have

Znk=\emptyset
for
k>n
and
Znn
is reduced to one element (the simplex

[n]
). The
standard hollow
n
-simplex

\partial Zn
is the
presimplicial set obtained from
Zn
by removing the top-dimensional simplex
in
Zn
, and there is a canonical inclusion
\partial Zn\hookrightarrow Zn
.

{defi}

A presimplicial
S\in\hat{\bigtriangleup}
set is
flag
if, for every integer

n\geqslant 3
and morphism
f:\partial Zn\to S
, there exists a unique morphism

g:Zn\to S
making the diagram

\textstyle{\partial Zn\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{S}
\textstyle{Zn\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}

commute, where the vertical arrow is the standard inclusion.

As previously, we sometimes say that a flag presimplicial set lifts uniquely the
inclusions
\partial Zn\hookrightarrow Zn
. In Theorem
18
, we
characterize
npc
precubical sets in terms of their links, and this will follow
from the following lemmas.

Lemma 14
.

Suppose given a precubical set
C
and a vertex
x\in C(0)
.

•

There is a bijection between presimplicial morphisms

\phi:Zn\to\operatorname{link}(x)
and pairs
(u,\psi)
with
u\in\left\{-,+\right\}^{n}
and

\psi:Y(n)\to C
a precubical morphism such that
\psi(u)=x
.

•

Similarly, there is a bijection between presimplicial morphisms

\phi:\partial Zn\to\operatorname{link}(x)
and all precubical morphisms of the form

\psi:\Lambda^{u}\to C
, for some
u\in\left\{-,+\right\}^{n}
, such that
\psi(u)=x
.

Proof 1.3
.

The first bijection follows easily from the Yoneda lemma: morphisms

\phi:Zn\to\operatorname{link}(x)
are in bijection with
n
-simplices in
\operatorname{link}(x)
, i.e. pairs
(u,y)
with
u\in\left\{-,+\right\}^{n}
and
y\in C(n)
such that

\partial^{u}(y)=x
, which are in turn in bijection with pairs
(u,\psi)
with

u\in\left\{-,+\right\}^{n}
and
\psi:Y(n)\to C
such that
\psi(u)=x
. The second
bijection is a slight variant of the first one.

Remark 15
.

It can moreover be checked that these bijections are compatible with
restriction to the border, what we will generally leave implicit in the
following. This means that given a presimplicial morphism
\phi:Zn\to\operatorname{link}(x)

corresponding to a precubical morphism
\psi:Y(n)\to C
by the first
bijection, the presimplicial morphism

\partial Zn\hookrightarrow Zn\overset{\phi}{\to}\operatorname{link}(x)
corresponds the the precubical
morphism
\partial Y(n)\hookrightarrow Y(n)\overset{\psi}{\to}C
by the second bijection.

The following lemma shows that
npc
precubical sets, always satisfy a
higher-dimensional generalization of the cube property.

Lemma 16
.

Suppose given an
npc
precubical set
C
. Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geqslant 3
and

u\in\left\{-,+\right\}^{n}
,
C
lifts uniquely the canonical embedding

\Lambda^{u}\hookrightarrow Yn
.

Proof 1.4
.

Given
u\in\left\{-,+\right\}^{n}
and
k
with
0\leqslant k\leqslant n
, we write
\Lambda^{u}_{k}

for the subcomplex of
Yn
whose cubes are the strings
v=\left\{-,0,+\right\}^{n}
which
either have one letter in common with
u
(i.e.
v_{i}=u_{i}
for some

i\in[n]
) or have at most
k
occurrences of the letter “
0
”. In
particular, we have
\Lambda^{u}_{0}=\Lambda^{u}
,
\Lambda^{u}_{n-1}=\partial Yn
and

\Lambda^{u}_{n}=Yn
. We show that
C
lifts uniquely the embedding

\Lambda^{u}\hookrightarrow\Lambda^{u}_{2}
, and the embeddings

\Lambda^{u}_{k}\hookrightarrow\Lambda^{u}_{k+1}
for
2\leqslant k<n
, from which we can
conclude. In order to illustrate our proof, we suppose that
u=-^{n}
(other
cases can be obtained by symmetry) and that
n=5
(but the arguments go
through similarly in the general case).

Case
k=0
. Suppose fixed a morphism
\phi:\Lambda^{u}\to C
. Given any triple
of indices
0\leqslant i_{0}<i_{1}<i_{2}<n
, there is an embedding

\lambda_{i_{0},i_{1},i_{2}}:\Lambda^{---}\hookrightarrow\Lambda^{u}
, sending a
k
-cube

v_{0}v_{1}v_{2}
to the
k
-cube

w\quad=\quad{+^{i_{0}}}v_{0}{+^{(i_{1}-i_{0}-1)}}v_{1}{+^{(i_{2}-i_{1}-i_{0}-2)}}v_{2}{+^{(n-3-i_{2})}}

i.e.
w
is obtained by inserting
v_{0}
,
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
at positions
i_{0}
,

i_{1}
and
i_{2}
respectively in the word constituted of
n-3
letters “
+
”:

w_{i}\quad=\quad\begin{cases}v_{i_{p}}&\text{if $i=i_{p}$ for some $p\in\left\{0,1,2\right\}$}\\
+&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

For instance, with
n=5
, we have
\lambda_{1,2,4}(v_{0}v_{1}v_{2})={+}v_{0}v_{1}{+}v_{2}

and the image of
\lambda_{1,2,4}
is shown on the left

\textstyle{{\scriptstyle+-++-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+-++0}
\scriptstyle{+0++-}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle+-+++}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+-0+0}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle+--+-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+--+0}
\scriptstyle{+-0+-}
\scriptstyle{0--}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle++++-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+00+-}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle+--++}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+-0++}
\scriptstyle{+0-++}
\scriptstyle{+0-+0}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle++-+-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{++-+0}
\scriptstyle{++0+-}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle++-++}

\textstyle{{\scriptstyle+-++-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+-++0}
\scriptstyle{+0++-}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle+-+++}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+0+++}
\scriptstyle{+0++0}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle++++-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{++++0}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle+--++}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+-0++}
\scriptstyle{+0-++}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle+++++}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+00++}
\scriptstyle{++0+0}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle+--}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{++-+0}
\scriptstyle{++0+-}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle++-++}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{++0++}

By the cube property, the morphism

\Lambda^{---}\hookrightarrow\Lambda^{u}\overset{\phi}{\to}C
extends to a morphism

\partial Y(n)\to C
(i.e. in the case of
\lambda_{1,2,4}
,
C
also
contains the other half-cube shown on the right above) and into
Y3
by the
unique 3-cube property. By the at most one square closing property (which holds by
Lemma
9
), the image of the image of the vertex
+^{n+1}

thus defined does not depend on the indices
i_{0},i_{1},i_{2}
. For instance, the
embeddings
\lambda_{1,2,4}
and
\lambda_{0,2,3}
can both be completed and
both define an image for the vertex
{\scriptstyle+++++}
: the one defined by

\lambda_{1,2,4}
is the same as the one defined by
\lambda_{1,2,3}
(the
completions share the face
{\scriptstyle+00++}
), which in turn is the same as the
one defined by
\lambda_{0,2,3}
(the completions share the face

{\scriptstyle++00+}
). Similarly, the 1- and 2-cubes defined by multiples embeddings
do not depend on the choice of the embedding. Therefore, the precubical
complex
C
lifts the embedding
\Lambda^{u}\hookrightarrow\Lambda^{u}_{2}
. Finally, by the
at most one square closing property again, the lifting is unique.

Suppose given
k
with
2\leqslant k<n
and a morphism
\phi:\Lambda^{u}_{k}\to C
. For
any
(k+1)
-uple of indices
0\leqslant i_{0}<\ldots<i_{k}<n
, there is an
embedding
\lambda_{i_{0},\ldots,i_{k}}:\partial Y(k+1)\hookrightarrow\Lambda^{u}_{k}
sending a
cube
v\in\left\{-,0,+\right\}^{k+1}
to the cube
w\in\left\{-,0,+\right\}^{n}
defined by

w_{i}\quad=\quad\begin{cases}v_{i_{p}}&\text{if $i=i_{p}$ for some $p\in[k+1]$}\\
+&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

By the unique
(k+1)
-cube property, the morphism

\partial Y(k+1)\hookrightarrow\Lambda^{u}_{k}\overset{\phi}{\to}C
extends uniquely to a
morphism
Y(k+1)\to C
. The precubical set
\Lambda^{u}_{k+1}
can be obtained
from
\Lambda^{u}_{k}
by properly adding
(k+1)
-cubes: more precisely, one can
easily check that
\Lambda^{u}_{k+1}
is the colimit of the diagram of
precubical sets

\textstyle{\Lambda^{u}_{k}}
\textstyle{Y(k+1)}
\textstyle{\partial Y(k+1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{I_{0}}}
\textstyle{\ldots}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\partial Y(k+1)}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{I_{p}}}

where the
I_{j}
range over subsets of cardinal
k+1
of
[n]
. Therefore, by
universal property of the colimit, the morphism
\phi
extends uniquely to a
morphism
\Lambda^{u}_{k+1}\to C
.

Notice that previous lemma also shows that the canonical embedding

\Lambda^{u}\hookrightarrow\partial Yn
is lifted uniquely. When the precubical set is only
supposed to be geometric, the lifting does not generally exists, but the proof
can easily be adapted in order to show that it is necessarily unique (the
particular case with
n=3
was already noticed in
Remark
7
):

Lemma 17
.

Suppose given a geometric precubical set
C
. Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geqslant 3

and
u\in\left\{-,+\right\}^{n}
,
C
lifts at most once the canonical embedding

\Lambda^{u}\hookrightarrow\partial Yn
.

Theorem 18
.

A geometric precubical set
C
is non-positively curved if and only if for
every vertex
x\in C(0)
, the presimplicial set
\operatorname{link}(x)
is flag.

Proof 1.5
.

We first show the left-to-right implication and suppose that
C
is
non-positively curved.
We show that for every vertex
x
of
C
the presimplicial set
\operatorname{link}(x)

lifts the inclusion
\partial Zn\hookrightarrow Zn
.
Suppose fixed a presimplicial morphism
\partial Zn\to\operatorname{link}(x)
. By
Lemma
14
, this amounts to fix a precubical morphism

\phi:\Lambda^{u}\to C
, for some
u\in\left\{-,+\right\}^{n}
, such that

\phi(u)=x
. By Lemma
16
, this morphism extends
uniquely to a morphism
\psi:Y(n)\to C
, satisfying
\psi(u)=x
, which
corresponds by Lemma
14
to a morphism
Zn\to\operatorname{link}(x)
.

We now show the right-to-left implication and suppose that
\operatorname{link}(x)
is flag
for every vertex
x
of
C
. We first show that
C
satisfies the cube
property. Suppose given
u\in\left\{-,+\right\}^{3}
and a precubical morphism

\phi:\Lambda^{u}\to C
, by Remark
13
we want to show that it
extends to a morphism
Y3\to C
(the uniqueness is granted by
Remark
7
). We write
x=\phi(u)
. By
Lemma
14
, the precubical morphism
\phi:\Lambda^{u}\to C

corresponds to a presimplicial morphism
\partial Z2\to\operatorname{link}(x)
, which can be
uniquely completed into a presimplicial morphism
Z2\to\operatorname{link}(x)
since the
link is flag, which corresponds to a morphism
Y3\to C
by
Lemma
14
, and therefore to a morphism
\partial Y3\to C

by precomposition with the inclusion
\partial Y3\hookrightarrow Y3
. We now show
that
C
satisfies the unique
(n)
-cube property for
n\geqslant 2
. Suppose
given a morphism
\phi:\partial Y(n)\to C
and write
x=\phi(u)
for some
vertex
u
of
\partial Y(n)
. By Lemma
14
, the
precubical morphism
\Lambda^{u}\hookrightarrow\partial Y(n)\overset{\phi}{\to}C

corresponds to a presimplicial morphism
\partial Zn\to\operatorname{link}(x)
, which
extends to a presimplicial morphism
Zn\to\operatorname{link}(x)
because the link is flag,
which in turn corresponds to a morphism
\psi:Y(n)\to C
by
Lemma
14
. By Remark
15
, we
have that the morphisms
\Lambda^{u}\hookrightarrow\partial Y(n)\overset{\phi}{\to}C
and

\Lambda^{u}\hookrightarrow Y(n)\overset{\psi}{\to}C
coincide, therefore the morphisms

\phi
and
\partial Y(n)\hookrightarrow Y(n)\overset{\psi}{\to}C
also coincide by
Lemma
17
. Finally, the lifting is
necessarily unique by Lemma
9
.

1.3.4.
2-dimensional non-positively curved precubical sets

Interestingly, a precubical set
C
satisfying the unique
n
-cube property, for
every
n\geqslant 3
, is characterized by its underlying 2-precubical set
C_{2}
(i.e. its image under the 2-truncation functor
\hat{\square}\to\hat{\square}_{2}
, see
Definition
1.1
). Higher-dimensional cubes can be recovered by a
“completion” process: the
3
-cubes can be recovered from
C_{2}
by adding a

3
-cube whenever
C_{2}
contains the border of a cube (i.e. for every morphism

\partial Y3\to C_{2}
), then the
4
-cubes can be obtained by adding a
4
-cube
whenever the precubical set contains the border of a
4
-cube, etc. More
precisely, by general results about presheaf categories, we have

Proposition 19
.

The 2-truncation functor
\hat{\square}\to\hat{\square}_{2}
admits a right adjoint

\hat{\square}_{2}\to\hat{\square}
, and this adjunction restricts to an equivalence of
categories between the category
\hat{\square}_{2}
and the full subcategory
of
\hat{\square}
whose objects are precubical sets satisfying the unique
n
-cube
property for every
n\geqslant 3
.

The image of a 2-precubical set under the right adjoint will be called its

completion
. All the constructions preformed here preserve the above
equivalence. For instance, we could easily define a “2-precubical semantics”
by adapting the definitions of Section
1.2
in order to associate a
2-precubical set to every program in the obvious way. It could then easily be
shown that the completion of the 2-precubical semantics is isomorphic to the
precubical semantics.

For this reason, we will mostly restrict, without loss of generality, to the underlying
2-precubical sets in the rest of the article.
In this setting, the property of being geometric can be also reformulated by
lifting properties, i.e. there is a reciprocal to Lemma
9
.

{defi}

Suppose given a 2-precubical set
C
.

•

We say that a precubical set
C
has
no looping edge
when there
is no edge
x\in C(1)
with the same source and target (i.e.
\partial^{-}(x)=\partial^{+}(x)
).

•

We say that it has
no folded square
when every square

x\in C(2)
is such that
\partial^{--}(x)\neq\partial^{++}(x)
and

\partial^{-+}(x)\neq\partial^{+-}(x)
.

•

We say that is has
no pinned squares
when for all squares

x,y\in C(2)
, either

–

\partial^{--}(x)=\partial^{--}(y)
and

\partial^{++}(x)=\partial^{++}(y)
, or

–

\partial^{+-}(x)=\partial^{+-}(y)
and

\partial^{-+}(x)=\partial^{-+}(y)

implies
x=y
.

A typical example of pinned square is given in Example
1.3.1
.

Lemma 20
.

A 2-precubical set
C
is geometric if and only if it has

(1)

no looping edge,

(2)

no folded square,

(3)

no parallel edges,

(4)

no pinned squares,

(5)

and the at most one square closing property.

Proof 1.6
.

Suppose given a 2-precubical set
C
satisfying the five above conditions.
The first condition says precisely that
C
has no self-intersecting edge. We
now show that it has no self-intersecting square. Suppose given
square
x\in C(2)
. Suppose that
x
has two 0-faces which are equal:

\partial^{\epsilon_{0}\epsilon_{1}}(x)=\partial^{\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}}(x)
. The
case
\epsilon_{0}\neq\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}
and
\epsilon_{1}\neq\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}
is excluded
because
C
has no folded square. Therefore we can suppose

\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}
(the case
\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}
is similar). If

\epsilon_{1}\neq\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}
then the edge
y=\partial^{\epsilon_{0}}_{0}
is
looping, which is forbidden, therefore
\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}
and the faces
are not distinct. Suppose that
x
has two 1-faces which are equal:

\partial^{\epsilon}_{i}(x)=\partial^{\epsilon^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime}}(x)
. If
i=i^{\prime}
then

\epsilon=\epsilon^{\prime}
, otherwise the edges
\partial^{-}_{1-i}(x)
and

\partial^{+}_{1-i}(x)
would both be looping. Suppose that
i\neq i^{\prime}
, then it
can easily be checked that this common face is a looping edge.
The fact that
C
satisfies the other conditions required for being geometric
can be checked by case analysis in a similar way, and the converse implication
is simple to check.

To sum up, in the rest of the paper, by a non-positively curved precubical set,
we can thus mean the following:

{defi}

A
non-positively curved
precubical set is a 2-precubical set which
satisfies

(1)

the five conditions of Lemma
20

(2)

the cube property.

2.
When dihomotopy coincides with homotopy

2.1.
Homotopy and dihomotopy in precubical sets

As exposed in the introduction, one of the main contributions of this article is
to show that homotopy coincides with its directed variant for paths in
npc
precubical sets. We begin by formally introducing these equivalences on paths,
which can be thought of as algebraic variants of the classical notion of
homotopy between paths in algebraic topology.
Since we will need to consider both directed and non-directed paths in
precubical sets
C
, we will consistently use the following terminology in the
remainder of the article. A
directed path
(or
dipath
) is a path in
the underlying directed graph of
C
(i.e. what we have been simply calling a
“path” up to now), and a
path
is a path in the underlying non-directed
graph
C
(i.e. a sequence of composable transitions which might contain
transitions taken backwards, that we call
reversed
transitions).
We sometimes write
s:x\twoheadrightarrow y
to indicate that
s
is a path with
x
as
source and
y
as target, and
\operatorname{id}_{x}:x\twoheadrightarrow x
for the empty path
on
x
.
The
length

\|s\|
of a path
s
is the number of (possibly reversed)
edges occurring in it. The concatenation of two paths
s:x\twoheadrightarrow y
and

t:y\twoheadrightarrow z
is written
s\cdot t:x\twoheadrightarrow z
, and the reversal of a
path
s:x\twoheadrightarrow y
is written
\overline{s}:y\twoheadrightarrow x
.
By an edge (or a transition)
a:x\to y
, we will always denote a dipath of
length one, and write
\overline{a}:y\to x
for the corresponding reversed transition,
unless we explicitly state that it can be possibly reversed, in which case we
denote a path of length one.

We conservatively extend the definition relation
\diamond

(Definition
1.1
) from dipaths to paths: given a precubical
set
C
, this relation is now defined as the smallest symmetric relation on
paths of length 2 such that

a\cdot b\diamond b^{\prime}\cdot a^{\prime}\qquad\qquad\overline{b}\cdot\overline{a}\diamond\overline{a^{\prime}}\cdot\overline{b^{\prime}}\qquad\qquad\overline{a}\cdot b^{\prime}\diamond b\cdot\overline{a^{\prime}}\qquad\qquad\overline{b^{\prime}}\cdot a\diamond a^{\prime}\cdot\overline{b}

(36)

whenever there is a square

\textstyle{x_{11}}
\textstyle{x_{10}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{\diamond}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces x_{01}}
\scriptstyle{a^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces x_{00}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{b^{\prime}}

(37)

in
C
.

{defi}

Given a precubical set
C
, the
dihomotopy
relation
\hat{\sim}
on paths
is the smallest congruence wrt concatenation containing
\diamond
. A

dihomotopy step
is a dihomotopy between two paths using only one of the
four above relations (
36
) in context.
The
homotopy
relation
\sim
on paths is the smallest congruence
containing
\hat{\sim}
and such that for every edge
a:x\to y
we have

a\cdot\overline{a}\sim\operatorname{id}_{x}\qquad\qquad\overline{a}\cdot a\sim\operatorname{id}_{y}

(38)

A
homotopy step
is a homotopy reduced to one of the six relations
(
36
) and (
38
) in context.

For geometric precubical sets, the notion of (di)homotopy coincides with the
usual topological one through the geometric realization, see
Section
2.3.3
and
[
fajstrup2005dipaths
]
.

By definition of the axioms defining dihomotopy, we have the following
properties.

Lemma 21
.

Two dihomotopic paths have the same length.

Lemma 22
.

A path which is dihomotopic to a dipath is necessarily a dipath.

These notions allow us to introduce variants of the well-known construction of
fundamental groupoid in algebraic topology.

{defi}

Given a precubical set
C
, its
fundamental groupoid

\Pi_{1}(C)
is
the category whose objects are vertices and morphisms are paths up to
homotopy, and its
fundamental category

\vec{\Pi}_{1}(C)
is the category
whose objects are vertices and morphisms are dipaths up to dihomotopy.

Since the relations (
38
) are precisely those required for every
transition
a
to admit
\overline{a}
as inverse, it is not hard to show that

Lemma 23
.

Given a precubical set
C
, the category
\Pi_{1}(C)
is the free groupoid
over the category
\vec{\Pi}_{1}(C)
.

By definition, two dipaths which are dihomotopic are homotopic. The main goal of
Section
2.2
is to show that for precubical sets satisfying the
cube property, the converse is true and both relations thus coincide
(Theorem
38
).

{exa}

Consider the standard hollow cube
\partial Y3
without the “bottom” face

{\scriptstyle 0-0}
, first considered in
[
fahrenberghomology
]
:

\textstyle{{\scriptstyle--+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0-+}
\scriptstyle{-0+}
\scriptstyle{00+}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\scriptstyle+-+}}
\scriptstyle{+0+}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle-++}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0++}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle+++}
\scriptstyle{-00}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle-+-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-+0}
\scriptstyle{0+-}
\scriptstyle{0+0}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\scriptstyle++-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{++0}
\scriptstyle{+00}
\textstyle{{\scriptstyle---}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{--0}
\scriptstyle{0--}
\scriptstyle{-0-}
\scriptstyle{00-}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\scriptstyle+--}}
\scriptstyle{+0-}
\scriptstyle{+-0}

The two external paths
{\scriptstyle--0}\cdot{\scriptstyle 0-+}
and
{\scriptstyle 0--}\cdot{\scriptstyle+-0}
are
homotopic (as detailed in Example
2.2.1
), but not
dihomotopic (each of those two paths is dihomotopic only to itself). Notice
that this precubical set does not satisfy the cube property.

We will also see in Section
3
that our algebraic conditions
for
npc
precubical sets are closely related to those characterizing
non-positively curved metric spaces in the traditional sense. Those spaces are
locally geodesic: in our context, a counterpart of this notion can be defined as
follows.

{defi}

A path
s
is
locally geodesic
if it is not dihomotopic to a path of
the form
s^{\prime}\cdot a\cdot\overline{a}\cdot s^{\prime\prime}
or
s^{\prime}\cdot\overline{a}\cdot a\cdot s^{\prime\prime}
. It is

geodesic
if every path homotopic to it has a greater or equal length.

By Lemma
22
, since the dihomotopy class of a dipath
contains only dipaths, the situation of previous definition can never occur:

Lemma 24
.

Every dipath is locally geodesic.

Clearly, a geodesic path is locally geodesic, but the reciprocal is not
generally true.

{exa}

Consider again the hollow cube without bottom face introduced in
Example
2.1
. The “loop around the hole”, i.e. the
path
{\scriptstyle--0}\cdot{\scriptstyle 0-+}\cdot\overline{{\scriptstyle+-0}}\cdot\overline{{\scriptstyle 0--}}
, is locally
geodesic. However it is not geodesic, since it is homotopic to the identity
path on the vertex
{\scriptstyle---}
, which is strictly shorter.

2.2.
A 2-categorical approach to dihomotopy in precubical sets

In this section, we reuse and extend the rewriting techniques developed by
Lafont
[
lafont2003towards
]
for presenting monoidal categories, and the
subsequent developments in the second author’s PhD thesis
[
mimram:phd
,
mimram2011structure
]
, in order to show one of the main results of this
article: homotopy and dihomotopy relations coincide for directed paths in
npc
precubical sets (Theorem
38
). This will be done by rewriting
homotopies between paths into a canonical form, which is always a dihomotopy
when the two paths are directed.

2.2.1.
The fundamental 2-category and 2-groupoid of a precubical set

In the following, we suppose fixed a 2-precubical set
C
which
satisfies:

(1)

the at most one square closing property,

(2)

the cube property,

(3)

for any square
\overline{a}\cdot a^{\prime}\diamond b\cdot\overline{b^{\prime}}
as in (
37
), we
have
a=a^{\prime}
if and only if
b=b^{\prime}
.

In particular any
npc
precubical set (see Definition
1.3.4
)
satisfies those conditions, which is our main object of interest here, but the
results of this section hold in this more general context.
To such a precubical set, we can canonically associate two distinct 2-categories
respectively defined as follows, which are 2-dimensional refinements of the
categories introduced in Definition
2.1
. The precise way in
which those are generalizations is given in Lemma
30
.

In classical algebraic topology, the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(X)
of a
topological space is constructed as the category with, as objects, the points of

X
, and as morphisms, the 1-tracks, or paths modulo homotopy. A direct
generalization is that of the fundamental bicategory, where points are still
0-cells, paths are 1-cells and 2-tracks or homotopies relative end points modulo
higher homotopies are 2-cells
[
Bigroupoid
]
. Composition in dimension 2, i.e. vertical composition is associative and even groupoidal (every 2-cell has an
inverse), but composition in dimension 1 is a little less well-behaved:
composition is only associative up to some 2-cells. Hence the natural structure
of
weak
2-category, or bicategory. In dimension one
also, the fundamental bicategory has all its 1-cells being invertible (up to
coherent 2-cells again), hence is a bigroupoid.
For Hausdorff spaces, it is possible to construct directly a fundamental
2-groupoid (hence a strict version)
[
2groupoid
]
, which is biequivalent to
the fundamental bigroupoid. We follow an equivalent approach here, in defining
a fundamental 2-category and fundamental 2-groupoid, but based on a
combinatorial structure, instead of a topological structure.

{defi}

The
fundamental 2-category

\vec{\Pi}_{2}(C)
associated to
C
is the
2-category whose

•

0-cells are the vertices of
C
,

•

1-cells are generated by (non-reversed) edges of
C
: 1-cells are
dipaths in
C
and composition is given by concatenation,

•

2-cells are freely generated by

\gamma^{a,b}_{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\quad:\quad a\cdot b\quad\Rightarrow\quad b^{\prime}\cdot a^{\prime}

whenever
a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
are transitions such that
a\cdot b\diamond b^{\prime}\cdot a^{\prime}
, and
quotiented by the smallest congruence (wrt both compositions) such that

\displaystyle(\gamma^{b^{\prime},c^{\prime}}_{c^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime}}\circ\operatorname{id}_{a^{\prime\prime}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{a^{\prime}}\cdot\gamma^{a^{\prime},c}_{c^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}})\circ(\gamma^{a,b}_{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{c})

\displaystyle\quad=\quad(\operatorname{id}_{c^{\prime\prime}}\cdot\gamma^{a^{\prime\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime\prime}}_{b^{\prime\prime},a^{\prime\prime}})\circ(\gamma^{a,c^{\prime\prime\prime}}_{c^{\prime\prime},a^{\prime\prime\prime}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{b^{\prime\prime\prime}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{a}\cdot\gamma^{b,c}_{c^{\prime\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime\prime}})

\displaystyle\gamma^{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}_{a,b}\circ\gamma^{a,b}_{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\operatorname{id}_{a\cdot b}

(39)

for transitions such that all the involved morphisms are
defined. Graphically,

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern \displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.83118pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{a}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.24167pt\raise 5.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{b}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 57.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}

The horizontal composition will be denoted as concatenation (
\cdot
), in
sequential order, whereas the vertical composition will be denoted as usual
(
\circ
), in categorical order, thus following the usual convention for
monoidal categories.

Remark 25
.

In the 2-category
\vec{\Pi}_{2}(C)
, if
\gamma^{a,b}_{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}
is defined, then
the transitions
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
are uniquely determined from
a
and
b

because
C
satisfies the at most one square closing property. Moreover, if

\gamma^{a,b}_{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}
is defined then
\gamma^{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}_{a,b}
is also defined,
and is an inverse for
\gamma^{a,b}_{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}
by (
39
): in
fact, the 2-category is a category enriched in groupoids.

Lemma 26
.

Two dipaths
f,g:x\twoheadrightarrow y
in
C
are dihomotopic if and only if there
exists a 2-cell
\alpha:f\Rightarrow g
in
\vec{\Pi}_{2}(C)
.

{defi}

The
fundamental 2-groupoid

\Pi_{2}(C)
associated to
C
is the
2-category whose

•

0-cells are the vertices of
C
,

•

1-cells are generated by edges of
C
or their reverse: 1-cells are
paths in
C
and composition is given by concatenation,

•

2-cells are freely generated by

\gamma^{a,b}_{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\quad:\quad a\cdot b\quad\Rightarrow\quad b^{\prime}\cdot a^{\prime}

whenever
a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
are possibly reversed transitions such that

a\cdot b\diamond b^{\prime}\cdot a^{\prime}
, and

\begin{array}[]{r@{\qquad:\qquad}r@{\qquad\To\qquad}l}\eta_{a}&\operatorname{id}_{x}\qquad\Rightarrow&a\cdot\overline{a}\\
\varepsilon_{a}&\overline{a}\cdot a\qquad\Rightarrow&\operatorname{id}_{y}\end{array}

whenever
a:x\to y
is a possibly reversed transition, and quotiented by the
smallest congruence (wrt both compositions) such that

\displaystyle(\gamma^{b^{\prime},c^{\prime}}_{c^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime}}\circ\operatorname{id}_{a^{\prime\prime}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{a^{\prime}}\cdot\gamma^{a^{\prime},c}_{c^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}})\circ(\gamma^{a,b}_{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{c})

\displaystyle\quad=\quad(\operatorname{id}_{c^{\prime\prime}}\cdot\gamma^{a^{\prime\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime\prime}}_{b^{\prime\prime},a^{\prime\prime}})\circ(\gamma^{a,c^{\prime\prime\prime}}_{c^{\prime\prime},a^{\prime\prime\prime}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{b^{\prime\prime\prime}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{a}\cdot\gamma^{b,c}_{c^{\prime\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime\prime}})

(40)

\displaystyle\gamma^{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}_{a,b}\circ\gamma^{a,b}_{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\operatorname{id}_{a\cdot b}

(41)

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}_{a}\cdot\varepsilon_{a})\circ(\eta_{a}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{a})

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\operatorname{id}_{a}

(42)

\displaystyle(\varepsilon_{a}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{\overline{a}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\overline{a}}\cdot\eta_{a})

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\operatorname{id}_{\overline{a}}

(43)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\overline{a}}\circ\eta_{a}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{id}}

(44)

\displaystyle\gamma^{a,\overline{a}}_{\overline{a^{\prime}},a^{\prime}}\circ\eta_{a}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\eta_{\overline{a^{\prime}}}

(45)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{a^{\prime}}\circ\gamma^{a,\overline{a}}_{\overline{a}^{\prime},a^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\varepsilon_{\overline{a}}

(46)

\displaystyle(\gamma^{a,b^{\prime}}_{b,a^{\prime}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{\overline{a^{\prime}}})\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{a}\cdot\gamma^{\overline{a},b}_{b^{\prime},\overline{a^{\prime}}})\circ(\eta_{a}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{b})

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\operatorname{id}_{b}\cdot\eta_{a^{\prime}}

(47)

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}_{a^{\prime}}\cdot\gamma^{b^{\prime},\overline{a}}_{\overline{a^{\prime}},b})\circ(\gamma^{b,a}_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{\overline{a}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{b}\cdot\eta_{a})

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\eta_{a^{\prime}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{b}

(48)

\displaystyle(\varepsilon_{a^{\prime}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{b})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\overline{a^{\prime}}}\cdot\gamma^{b^{\prime},a}_{a^{\prime},b})\circ(\gamma^{b,\overline{a}}_{\cdot}\operatorname{id}_{a})

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\operatorname{id}_{b}\cdot\varepsilon_{a}

(49)

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}_{b}\cdot\varepsilon_{a^{\prime}})\circ(\gamma^{\overline{a},b^{\prime}}_{b,\overline{a^{\prime}}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{a^{\prime}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\overline{a}}\cdot\gamma^{a,b}_{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\varepsilon_{a}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{b}

(50)

for possibly reversed transitions
a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime},c,c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime}
such that all
the involved morphisms are defined.

In string diagrammatic notation, the generators are drawn as

or often simply as

and the relations of Definition
2.2.1
can be pictured as

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{yb_r.pdf}}}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{sym_r.pdf}}}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{zigzag1_r.pdf}}}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{zigzag2_r.pdf}}}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{etasym_r.pdf}}}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{epssym_r.pdf}}}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{etanat1_r.pdf}}}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{etanat2_r.pdf}}}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{epsnat1_r.pdf}}}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{epsnat2_r.pdf}}}

By definition of the generating 2-cells, we have that

Lemma 27
.

Two paths
f,g:x\twoheadrightarrow y
in
C
are homotopic if and only if there exists a
2-cell
\alpha:f\Rightarrow g
in
\Pi_{2}(C)
.

{exa}

In the hollow cube without bottom
C
of Example
2.1
,
the fact that the two paths
f={\scriptstyle--0}\cdot{\scriptstyle 0-+}
and

g={\scriptstyle 0--}\cdot{\scriptstyle+-0}
are homotopic is witnessed by the following 2-cell

\phi:f\Rightarrow g
in
\Pi_{2}(C)
(drawn from left to right instead of top to
bottom for space constraints):

\phi\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{hollow-cube-hom.pdf}}}

Of course, this precubical set does not satisfy our assumptions, but we could
still define a fundamental 2-category, excepting that some members of the
equations may not be defined. We allow ourselves to consider it in this
example only, for illustrative purposes.

Proposition 28
.

The following relations are derivable for 2-cells in
\Pi_{2}(C)
:

\displaystyle(\gamma^{b,a^{\prime}}_{a,b^{\prime}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{\overline{a^{\prime}}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{b}\cdot\eta_{a^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\quad=\quad(\operatorname{id}_{a}\cdot\gamma^{\overline{a},b}_{b^{\prime},\overline{a^{\prime}}})\circ(\eta_{a}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{b})

(51)

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}_{b^{\prime}}\cdot\varepsilon_{a^{\prime}})\circ(\gamma^{\overline{a},b}_{b^{\prime},\overline{a^{\prime}}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{a^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\quad=\quad(\varepsilon_{a}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{b^{\prime}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\overline{a}}\cdot\gamma^{b,a^{\prime}}_{a,b^{\prime}})

(52)

\displaystyle(\varepsilon_{a^{\prime}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{a})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\overline{a^{\prime}}}\cdot\gamma^{a^{\prime},a}_{a^{\prime},a})\circ(\eta_{\overline{a^{\prime}}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{a})

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\operatorname{id}_{a}

(53)

(when the involved morphisms are defined). Graphically,

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{etaturn_r.pdf}}}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{epsturn_r.pdf}}}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{etasymeps_r.pdf}}}

Proof 2.1
.

We provide graphical derivations for the first and third relations:

\begin{array}[]{c}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{etaturn1.pdf}}}\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{etaturn2.pdf}}}\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{etaturn3.pdf}}}\\
\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{etasymeps1.pdf}}}\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{etasymeps2.pdf}}}\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{etasymeps3.pdf}}}\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{etasymeps4.pdf}}}\end{array}

It can easily be checked that the intermediate morphisms are always defined
when the first and last one are. For instance, the equational derivation of
the first relation is

(\gamma^{b,a^{\prime}}_{a,b^{\prime}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{\overline{a^{\prime}}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{b}\cdot\eta_{a^{\prime}})=(\operatorname{id}_{a}\cdot\gamma^{\overline{a},b}_{b^{\prime},\overline{a^{\prime}}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{a}\cdot\gamma^{b^{\prime},\overline{a^{\prime}}}_{\overline{a},b})\circ(\gamma^{b,a^{\prime}}_{a,b^{\prime}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{\overline{a^{\prime}}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{b}\cdot\eta_{a^{\prime}})=(\operatorname{id}_{a}\cdot\gamma^{\overline{a},b}_{b^{\prime},\overline{a^{\prime}}})\circ(\eta_{a}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{b})

Since

\gamma^{b,a^{\prime}}_{a,b^{\prime}}
is supposed to be defined, we have

b\cdot a^{\prime}\diamond a\cdot b^{\prime}
in
C
and therefore the morphisms

\gamma^{b^{\prime},\overline{a^{\prime}}}_{\overline{a},b}
and
\gamma^{\overline{a},b}_{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}
are also
defined. The relation (
52
) is similar to (
51
).

Remark 29
.

The generators (for 0-, 1- and 2-cells) of
\vec{\Pi}_{2}(C)
are a subset of the
generators of
\Pi_{2}(C)
and similarly for relations. There is thus a
canonical functor
\vec{\Pi}_{2}(C)\to\Pi_{2}(C)
exhibiting a quotient of

\vec{\Pi}_{2}(C)
as a subcategory of
\Pi_{2}(C)
. In the following we study
some of the properties of this functor.

The interest of those 2-categories is that they respectively contain “more
information” than the fundamental category and groupoid, in the following
sense. Given a 2-category
\mathcal{C}
, we write
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
for the category with
the 0-cells of
\mathcal{C}
as objects, and morphisms are the 1-cells of
\mathcal{C}
quotiented
by the smallest equivalence relation identifying two 1-cells between which there
exists a 2-cell.

Lemma 30
.

We have
\vec{\Pi}_{1}(C)\cong\widetilde{\vec{\Pi}_{2}(C)}
and

\Pi_{1}(C)\cong\widetilde{\Pi_{2}(C)}
.

2.2.2.
Rewriting homotopies as dihomotopies

By definition, the 2-cells of
\Pi_{2}(C)
are equivalence classes of 2-cells
in the free 2-category generated by the 2-cells

\gamma^{a,b}_{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}
,
\eta_{a}
and
\varepsilon_{a}
, quotiented by the
equivalence relation
\equiv
generated by the relations. An element of such an
equivalence class is called a
formal 2-cell
. We say that a formal 2-cell

\phi

rewrites
to a 2-cell
\psi
, which we write
\phi\Rrightarrow\psi
, when

\psi
can be obtained from
\phi
by iteratively replacing the left member of a
relation in some context by the right member of the relation in the same
context, where the relation is one of the eleven relations of
Definition
2.2.1
or the three relations of
Proposition
28
. For instance the formal 2-cell on the left
rewrites to the formal 2-cell on the right using the relation
(
41
):

\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{etaturn2.pdf}}}\qquad\Rrightarrow\qquad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{etaturn1.pdf}}}

More formally, we can introduce a 3-category with the above free 2-category as
underlying 2-category, and 3-cells generated by the relations oriented from left
to right, and we would have
\phi\Rrightarrow\psi
precisely when there exists a 3-cell
from
\phi
to
\psi
in this 3-category. The interested reader can find a more
detailed presentation of this construction in
[
t3rt
]
. Notice that if
\phi

and
\psi
are two formal 2-cells such that
\phi\Rrightarrow\psi
then
\phi
and
\psi

are in the same equivalence class, moreover the following lemma ensures that
rewriting a formal 2-cell will always produce a (well-defined) formal 2-cell:

Lemma 31
.

In Definition
2.2.1
and Proposition
28
, if
the left member of a relation is well-defined then the right member is also
well-defined.

Proof 2.2
.

We show that when the left member of a relation is well-defined then the right
member is also well-defined. The property is verified for
(
40
) because
C
is supposed to satisfy the cube property,
and the right members of other relations of Definition
2.2.1
are
always well-defined. For (
51
), if the left-member is
well-defined then
\gamma^{b,a^{\prime}}_{a,b^{\prime}}
is, i.e.
b\cdot a^{\prime}\diamond a\cdot b^{\prime}
in
C
,
and therefore
\gamma^{\overline{a},b}_{b^{\prime},\overline{a^{\prime}}}
is well-defined. The case
of (
52
) is similar, and the right-member of (
53
)
is always defined.

We call a
slice
a formal 2-cell
\phi
of the form

\phi=\operatorname{id}_{f}\cdot\alpha\cdot\operatorname{id}_{g}
where
f,g
are 1-cells and
\alpha
is a
generating 2-cell. A slice is thus a 2-cell constituted of a unique generator in
identity context:

Using the laws of 2-categories it is easy to show the following lemma, which
will enable us to reason by induction on formal 2-cells:

Lemma 32
.

Any formal 2-cell
\phi
can be expressed as a composite of slices: there
exists slices
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n}
such that

\phi=\phi_{n}\circ\ldots\circ\phi_{1}
. Moreover, any two such decompositions
have the same number of slices.

{defi}

The number
n
of slices in the decomposition of a formal 2-cell
\phi
is
called its
length
and is denoted
\|\phi\|
.

We first generalize the notation for the 2-cells
\gamma^{a,b}_{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}
as
follows:

{defi}

Given transitions
a,a^{\prime}
and 1-cells
f,f^{\prime}
, we write

\gamma^{a,f}_{f^{\prime},a^{\prime}}:a\cdot f\Rightarrow f^{\prime}\cdot a^{\prime}
for the 2-cell defined by induction
on the length of
f
by

•

if
a:x\to y
then

\gamma^{a,\operatorname{id}_{y}}_{\operatorname{id}_{x},a}\quad=\quad\operatorname{id}_{a}

•

given transitions
a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
such that
a\cdot b\diamond b^{\prime}\cdot a^{\prime}
, we have

\gamma^{a,b\cdot f}_{b^{\prime}\cdot f^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}}\quad=\quad(\gamma^{a,b}_{b^{\prime}a^{\prime}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{f})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{b^{\prime}}\cdot\gamma^{a^{\prime},f}_{f^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}})

whenever
\gamma^{a^{\prime},f}_{f^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}}
is defined.

Graphically, we have

\gamma^{a,b_{1}\cdot b_{2}\cdot\ldots\cdot b_{n}}_{b_{1}^{\prime}\cdot b_{2}^{\prime}\cdot\ldots\cdot b_{n}^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{gamman.pdf}}}

As explained in the beginning of the section, we are going to show that every
formal 2-cell can be rewritten into one of a particular form, called a canonical
form. For those, we will be able to show that they are actually homotopies when
the source and target are directed paths (Lemma
37
) and
conclude.

{defi}

A formal 2-cell
\phi
is a
canonical form
when it is of the form

\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{id}_{x}}
for some 0-cell
x
, what we write
Z_{x}
, or there exists a
canonical form
\psi
such that
\phi
is of one of the four following forms:

\displaystyle G^{a,f,g}_{f^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\psi

\displaystyle\quad=\quad(\gamma^{a,f}_{f^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{g})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{a}\cdot\psi)

\displaystyle H^{f,a,g,h}_{g^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\psi

\displaystyle\quad=\quad(\operatorname{id}_{f\cdot a}\cdot\gamma^{\overline{a},g}_{g^{\prime},\overline{a^{\prime}}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{h})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{f}\cdot\eta_{a}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{g\cdot h})\circ\psi

\displaystyle E^{a,f}\psi

\displaystyle\quad=\quad(\varepsilon_{a}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{f})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\overline{a}}\cdot\psi)

for some transitions
a
,
a^{\prime}
and morphisms
f
,
f^{\prime}
,
g
,
g^{\prime}
,
h
.
Graphically,

\displaystyle Z_{x}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad

\displaystyle H^{f,a,g,h}_{g^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\psi

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{H.pdf}}}

\displaystyle G^{a,f,g}_{f^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\psi

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{G.pdf}}}

\displaystyle E^{a,f}\psi

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{E.pdf}}}

The operations
G
,
H
and
E
on 2-cells are called
operators
.
Notice that the operator
E^{a,f}
transforms a 2-cell
\psi:f\Rightarrow\overline{a}\cdot g
into
a 2-cell
E^{a,f}\psi:a\cdot f\Rightarrow g
. Similarly, the “type” for other operators is

\begin{array}[]{r@{\qquad:\qquad}r@{\qTo}l@{\qquad\rightsquigarrow\qquad}r@{\qTo}l}G^{a,f,g}_{f^{\prime},a^{\prime}}&h\quad\Rightarrow\quad&f\cdot g&a\cdot h\quad\Rightarrow\quad&f^{\prime}\cdot a^{\prime}\cdot g\\
H^{f,a,g,h}_{g^{\prime},a^{\prime}}&i\quad\Rightarrow\quad&f\cdot g\cdot h&i\quad\Rightarrow\quad&f\cdot a\cdot g^{\prime}\cdot\overline{a^{\prime}}\cdot h\\
E^{a,f}&g\quad\Rightarrow\quad&\overline{a}\cdot f&a\cdot g\quad\Rightarrow\quad&f\end{array}

We sometimes use the notation
I^{a,f}
for the operator
G^{a,\operatorname{id},f}_{\operatorname{id},a,f}
:

I^{a,f}\psi\quad=\quad G^{a,\operatorname{id},f}_{\operatorname{id},a,f}\psi\quad=\quad\operatorname{id}_{a}\cdot\psi\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{I.pdf}}}

and simply write
I^{a}
when
f
is clear from the context.

Proposition 33
.

Every formal 2-cell
\phi
rewrites to a canonical form.

Proof 2.3
.

By induction on the length of
\phi
. If
\|\phi\|=0
, then

\phi=\operatorname{id}_{f}=I^{a_{1}}\cdots I^{a_{n}}Z
for some 1-cell
f=a_{1}\cdots a_{n}
and we
conclude. Otherwise
\|\phi\|=n+1
, and by Lemma
32
,
\phi

admits a decomposition as
\phi=\sigma\circ\psi
with
\|\psi\|=n
and

\sigma
is a slice, i.e.
\sigma=\operatorname{id}_{f}\cdot\alpha\cdot\operatorname{id}_{g}
for some generating
2-cell
\alpha
and 1-cells
f
and
g
. By induction hypothesis,
\psi
is
equal to a canonical form, and we conclude by examining all the possible forms
for the canonical form
\psi
and the slice
\sigma
.

•

Suppose that
\alpha=\eta_{a}
. Then

\phi=(\operatorname{id}_{f}\cdot\eta_{a}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{g})\circ\psi=H^{f,a,\operatorname{id},g}_{\operatorname{id},a}\psi
.

•

Suppose that
\alpha=\varepsilon_{b}
. We proceed by induction on
\psi
.

–

Suppose that
\psi=G^{a,f^{\prime\prime},g^{\prime}}_{f^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\psi^{\prime}
:

\psi=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{Gproof.pdf}}}

Depending on
f
and
g
, the following cases are possible.

*

If
f^{\prime}=f^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\overline{b}\cdot b\cdot f^{\prime}_{2}
,
f=f^{\prime}_{1}
and
g=f^{\prime}_{2}\cdot a^{\prime}\cdot g^{\prime}
,

\phi=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{cf-proof1a.pdf}}}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{cf-proof1b.pdf}}}\Rrightarrow\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{cf-proof1c.pdf}}}=G\psi^{\prime\prime}

where
\psi^{\prime\prime}
is a canonical form obtained by induction.

*

If
f^{\prime}=f^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\overline{a^{\prime}}
,
b=a^{\prime}
,
f=f^{\prime}_{1}
and
g=g^{\prime}
,

\phi=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{cf-proof2a.pdf}}}\Rrightarrow\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{cf-proof2b.pdf}}}\Rrightarrow\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{cf-proof2c.pdf}}}\Rrightarrow\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{cf-proof2d.pdf}}}=E\psi^{\prime\prime}

where
\psi^{\prime\prime}
is a canonical form obtained by induction.

*

If
g^{\prime}=\overline{a^{\prime}}\cdot g^{\prime}_{2}
,
b=\overline{a^{\prime}}
,
f=f^{\prime}
,
g=g^{\prime}_{2}
,

\phi=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{cf-proof3a.pdf}}}\Rrightarrow\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{cf-proof3b.pdf}}}\Rrightarrow\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{cf-proof3c.pdf}}}=E\psi^{\prime\prime}

where
\psi^{\prime\prime}
is a canonical form obtained by induction.

*

If
g^{\prime}=g^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\overline{b}\cdot b\cdot g^{\prime}_{2}

\phi=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{cf-proof4a.pdf}}}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{cf-proof4b.pdf}}}\Rrightarrow\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{cf-proof4c.pdf}}}=G\psi^{\prime\prime}

where
\psi^{\prime\prime}
is a canonical form obtained by induction.

–

The cases where
\psi=H^{f,a,g,h}_{g^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\psi^{\prime}
and

\psi=E^{a,f}\psi^{\prime}
can be handled similarly by case analysis.

•

The case where
\alpha=\gamma^{a,b}_{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}
can be handled similarly by
case analysis.

A canonical form is not, in general, a normal form. The following lemma shows
that a canonical form of the form
XEHY
(where
X
and
Y
are composites of
operators and we omit indices) can always be rewritten to a canonical form of
the form
XGY
of
XHEY
, and
XGHY
to
XHGY
. By severely abusing notations,
this can be summarized as

EH\quad\Rrightarrow\quad G\text{ or }HE\qquad\qquad\qquad GH\quad\Rrightarrow\quad HG

From these relations, it is easy to show that if a canonical form contains a
H

(i.e. is of the form
XHY
) then it can be rewritten to one which contains a
H

in first position (i.e. of the form
HX
) and if it contains an
E
(i.e. is of
the form
XEY
) then it can be rewritten to one of the form
XEY
where
Y
does
not contain an
H
. This will be used in Lemma
37
.

Lemma 34
.

The following rewriting relations can be shown:

(1)
for every morphism
\phi:i\to g\cdot h
,

\displaystyle E^{a,g^{\prime}\cdot\overline{a^{\prime}}\cdot h}H^{\operatorname{id},a,g,h}_{g^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\phi

\displaystyle\quad\Rrightarrow\quad G^{a,g,h}_{g^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\phi

\displaystyle\quad\Rrightarrow\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{EH2.pdf}}}

(2)
for every morphism
\phi:i\to b\cdot f\cdot g\cdot h
,

\displaystyle E^{b,f\cdot a\cdot g^{\prime}\cdot\overline{a^{\prime}}\cdot h}H^{b\cdot f,a,g,h}_{g^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\phi

\displaystyle\quad\Rrightarrow\quad H^{f,a,g,h}_{g^{\prime},a^{\prime}}E^{b,f\cdot g\cdot h}\phi

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{EHcom2.pdf}}}

(3)
for every morphism
\phi:i\Rightarrow f_{1}\cdot f_{2}\cdot g\cdot h
,

\displaystyle G^{b,f_{1},f_{2}\cdot a^{\prime}\cdot g^{\prime}\cdot\overline{a^{\prime}}\cdot h}_{f_{1}^{\prime},b^{\prime}}H^{f_{1}\cdot f_{2},a,g,h}_{g^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\phi

\displaystyle\quad\Rrightarrow\quad H^{f_{1}^{\prime}\cdot b^{\prime}\cdot f_{2},a,g,h}_{g^{\prime},a^{\prime}}G^{b,f_{1},f_{2}\cdot g\cdot h}_{f_{1}^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\phi

\displaystyle\quad=\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{GHcom2.pdf}}}

(4)
for every morphism
\phi:i\Rightarrow f\cdot g_{1}\cdot g_{2}\cdot h

\displaystyle G^{b,f\cdot a\cdot g_{1}^{\prime\prime},g_{2}^{\prime}\cdot\overline{a^{\prime\prime}}\cdot h}_{f^{\prime}\cdot a^{\prime}\cdot g_{1}^{\prime},b^{\prime}}H^{b\cdot f,a,g_{1}\cdot g_{2},h}_{g_{1}^{\prime\prime}\cdot g_{2}^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}}\phi

\displaystyle\quad\Rrightarrow\quad H^{f^{\prime},a^{\prime},g_{2}^{\prime\prime}\cdot b^{\prime\prime}\cdot g_{2},h}_{g_{1}^{\prime}\cdot b^{\prime}\cdot g_{2}^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}}G^{b,f\cdot g_{1},g_{2}\cdot h}_{f^{\prime}\cdot g_{2}^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime}}\phi

\displaystyle\quad\Rrightarrow\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{GHmid2.pdf}}}

(5)
for every morphism
\phi:i\Rightarrow f\cdot g\cdot h_{1}\cdot h_{2}

\displaystyle G^{b,f\cdot a\cdot g^{\prime\prime}\cdot\overline{a^{\prime\prime\prime}}\cdot h_{1},h_{2}}_{f^{\prime}\cdot a^{\prime}\cdot g^{\prime}\cdot\overline{a^{\prime\prime}}\cdot h_{1}^{\prime},b^{\prime}}H^{b\cdot f,a,g,h_{1}\cdot h_{2}}_{g^{\prime\prime},a^{\prime\prime\prime}}\phi

\displaystyle\quad\Rrightarrow\quad H^{f^{\prime},a^{\prime},g^{\prime\prime\prime},h_{1}^{\prime}\cdot b^{\prime}\cdot h_{2}}_{g^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}}G^{b,f\cdot g\cdot h_{1},h_{2}}_{f^{\prime}\cdot g^{\prime\prime\prime}\cdot h_{1}^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\phi

\displaystyle\quad\Rrightarrow\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{GHr2.pdf}}}

(in the last two cases the omitted indices can be inferred from the figure).

Proof 2.4
.

(1) is a direct application of relation (
43
), (2) and
(3) are equalities (they follow from the exchange law in 2-categories), (4)
and (5) can be shown by applying suitable sequence of rewriting, which can
easily be guessed from the figures.

In the following, we sometimes omit the superscript and subscript indices of
operators for simplicity. We say that a 1-cell
f

contains a reversed
transition
when it can be written in the form
f=f_{1}\cdot\overline{a}\cdot f_{2}
where

\overline{a}
is a reversed transition: a path
f
is a dipath when it does not
contain a reversed transition. Two possibly reversed transitions
a
and
a^{\prime}

are said to have the
same direction
if they are both non-reversed or both
reversed.

Lemma 35
.

In a 2-cell of the form
\gamma^{a,b}_{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}
the transitions
a
and
a^{\prime}

(resp.
b
and
b^{\prime}
) have the same direction. In a 2-cell of the form

\gamma^{a,f}_{f^{\prime},a^{\prime}}
the transitions
a
and
a^{\prime}
have the same direction,
and
f
contains a reversed transition if and only if
f^{\prime}
does.

Proof 2.5
.

The first point is immediate by definition of
\gamma^{a,b}_{b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}
. This can
easily be used to show the result on
\gamma^{a,f}_{f^{\prime},a^{\prime}}
by induction on its
definition (Definition
2.2.2
).

Lemma 36
.

The source and target of a formal 2-cell in canonical form satisfy the
following properties.

(1)

A formal 2-cell of the form
H\phi
necessarily contains a reversed
transition in its target.

(2)

Z
contains no reversed transition in its source or its target.

(3)

G\phi
contains a reversed transition in its target if and only if
either it contains a reversed transition in its source or
\phi
contains a
reversed transition in its target.

(4)

A formal 2-cell of the form
EX
where is consists of operators
within
\left\{Z,E,G\right\}
necessarily contains a reversed transition in its
source.

Proof 2.6
.

The properties can be shown as follows.

(1)

The target of a formal 2-cell
H^{f,a,g,h}_{a^{\prime},g^{\prime}}
is

f\cdot a\cdot g^{\prime}\cdot\overline{a^{\prime}}\cdot h
and thus contains a reversed transition since
it contains both
a
and
\overline{a^{\prime}}
, and
a
and
a^{\prime}
have the same direction
(this follows easily from Lemma
35
).

(2)

The source (resp. target) of
Z
is an empty path.

(3)

Consider
G^{a,f,g}_{f^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\phi:a\cdot h\Rightarrow f^{\prime}\cdot a^{\prime}\cdot g
with

\phi:h\Rightarrow f\cdot g
, where

G^{a,f,g}_{f^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\phi=(\gamma^{a,f}_{f^{\prime},a^{\prime}}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{g})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{a}\cdot\phi)
. If
the transition
a^{\prime}
is reversed then
a
is also reversed and we
conclude. Otherwise, either
f^{\prime}
or
g
contains a reversed transition. If
it the case for
g
then
\phi
contains a reversed transition in its
target. Otherwise
f^{\prime}
contains a reversed transition and thus also
f
by
previous lemma. The converse implication is similar.

(4)

It is enough to show the property in the case where
X
is of the form

X=GG\ldots GZ
because if
EX
contains a reversed transition in its source
then so does
EX^{\prime}EX
. Consider
E^{a,f}\phi:\overline{a}\cdot g\Rightarrow f
with

\phi:g\to a\cdot f
, if
a
not reversed then
E^{a,f}\phi
contains the
reversed transition
\overline{a}
in its source. Otherwise
\phi
contains a
reversed transition in its target and is of the form
GG\ldots GZ
. By an
easy induction, using the two previous cases, it thus contains a reversed
transition in its source. The converse implication is similar.

Lemma 37
.

Suppose given a formal 2-cell
\phi:f\Rightarrow g
whose source
f
and target
g

are dipaths (i.e. do not contain reversed transitions). Then
\phi
rewrites to
a formal 2-cell which is a composite of generators not involving generators of
the form
\eta_{a}
and
\varepsilon_{a}
.

Proof 2.7
.

By Proposition
33
,
\phi
rewrites to a canonical form. This
canonical form corresponds to a formal 2-cell involving a generator of the
form
\eta_{a}
if and only if it contains an operator
H
. By
Lemma
34
, up to more rewriting, if the canonical form
contains an operator
H
we can always suppose that there is one placed at
leftmost position. This situation is impossible because a morphism of the form

H\psi
necessarily contains a reversed transition in its target by
Lemma
36
. Similarly, the formal 2-cell involves a generator

\varepsilon_{a}
if and only if its canonical form contains an operator
E
. Up
to rewriting more, we can always suppose that the canonical form is of the
form
YEX
where
X
consists of operators not involving
H
. By
Lemma
36
, it thus contains a reversed transition in its
source, contradicting the hypothesis.

Theorem 38
.

Two dipaths in
C
are homotopic if and only if they are dihomotopic.

Proof 2.8
.

We show the left-to-right implication, the converse being obvious. Suppose
that
f
and
g
are two homotopic dipaths. By Lemma
27
,
this means that there exists a 2-cell
\phi:f\Rightarrow g
in
\Pi_{2}(C)
. This
2-cell
\phi
is, by definition, an equivalence class of formal 2-cells under
the relations defining fundamental 2-groupoids. Choose an arbitrary formal
2-cell
\psi
in this equivalence class. By Lemma
37
,
\psi

rewrite to a formal 2-cell
\psi^{\prime}
, which is still in the equivalence
class
\phi
by definition of rewriting, and does not involve
generators
\eta
or
\varepsilon
. Therefore, the 2-cell
\phi
is in the
image of the canonical functor
\vec{\Pi}_{2}(C)\to\Pi_{2}(C)
(see
Remark
29
), and there exists a dihomotopy from
f

to
g
by Lemma
26
.

This can be formulated more categorically as follows.

Theorem 39
.

The quotient functor
\vec{\Pi}_{1}(C)\to\Pi_{1}(C)
is full and faithful.

On a related note, a characterization of the embeddability of small categories
into their groupoid completion is given in
[
johnstone2008embedding
]
, and
variants of the above theorem have also been investigated in more geometric
contexts, see the references given in Section
.

2.3.
Extensions

In this section, we briefly comment on a few topics and extensions related to
the preceding approach. We omit proofs, they should be detailed in subsequent
works.

2.3.1.
The free compact closed category on a unidimensional object

Given an
npc
precubical set
C
, we have seen (Lemma
27
) that
homotopy corresponds to the existence of a 2-cell in
\Pi_{2}(C)
and
therefore the relations we put on 2-cells in the definition of
\Pi_{2}(C)
do
not really matter as long as they are well-defined
(Lemma
31
) and allow us to rewrite a homotopy between
dipaths to a dihomotopy (Theorem
38
). In this section, we
advocate that our axioms are not completely arbitrary by showing that our
construction encompasses the free compact closed category on an unidimensional
object as a particular case.

We study here the particular case of the 2-category
\mathcal{C}=\Pi_{2}(C)
where
C

is the precubical set with one vertex
x
, one edge
a:x\to x
and one square

a\cdot a\diamond a\cdot a
:

C\quad=\quad\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 68.43054pt\raise-57.74997pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}

This will illustrate that our axioms for the category
\vec{\Pi}_{2}(C)
constitute
a variant of the well-known notion of compact-closed category. Since the
2-category
\mathcal{C}
has only one 0-cell, we can consider it as a monoidal category,
with “
\cdot
” as tensor product. It can be noticed that the monoidal
category
\mathcal{C}
is symmetric (the symmetry being generated by

\gamma^{a,a}_{a,a}:a\cdot a\to a\cdot a
), and compact closed (with
\overline{a}
being
dual to
a
):

{defi}

A symmetric monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\cdot,I)
is
compact closed
when for
every object
A\in\mathcal{C}
, there exists an object
A^{*}\in\mathcal{C}
, called the

dual
of
A
, together with two morphisms

\eta_{A}:I\to A\cdot A^{*}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\varepsilon_{A}:A^{*}\cdot A\to A

such that

(\operatorname{id}_{A}\cdot\varepsilon_{A})\circ(\eta_{A}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{A})=\operatorname{id}_{A}\qquad\qquad(\varepsilon_{A}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{A^{*}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{A^{*}}\cdot\eta_{A})=\operatorname{id}_{A^{*}}

An object
A\in\mathcal{C}
in such a category is
unidimensional
when

\varepsilon_{A}\circ\gamma_{A,A^{*}}\circ\eta_{A}=\operatorname{id}_{I}
.

{exa}

Consider the symmetric monoidal category
