2.3.2.
Convergence of the rewriting system

We conjecture that this rewriting system is convergent, i.e. both terminating and
confluent. We believe that Guiraud’s techniques based on
derivations
[
guiraud2006termination
]
can be used in order to show the
termination of the rewriting system. Confluence is quite tedious to check.
Because of the “Yang-Baxter” rule (
40
) there is an infinite
number of critical pairs as discovered by Lafont
[
lafont2003towards
]
. We
could still be able to handle those families of critical pairs (as done by
Lafont), however this requires beforehand to establish the existence of
canonical forms as we did in previous section: it would therefore require
strictly more work than done here. As a byproduct of this result, we expect to
be able to show that in
\Pi_{2}(C)
(and therefore also in
\vec{\Pi}_{2}(C)
by
Theorem
39
) there is at most one homotopy between any two
paths (of course, when
C
satisfies the cube property and other hypothesis).

2.3.3.
An axiomatization of homotopy between homotopies

As explained in detail in next section, one can interpret a precubical set
C

as a directed space
\mathopen{\upharpoonleft}C\mathclose{\upharpoonright}
by taking its geometric realization (see
Definition
). It has been shown by
Fajstrup
[
fajstrup2005dipaths
]
that two paths in
C
are dihomotopic (resp. homotopic) in
C
if and only if the corresponding paths in
\mathopen{\upharpoonleft}C\mathclose{\upharpoonright}
are
dihomotopic (resp. homotopic) in the geometric sense, i.e. our algebraic notion
of (di)homotopy corresponds to the geometric one. We conjecture that this result
extends one dimension higher, i.e. that two formal 2-cells in
\Pi_{2}(C)

(resp.
\vec{\Pi}_{2}(C)
) are equal (i.e. in the same equivalence class modulo the
relations) if and only if the corresponding homotopies (resp. dihomotopies)
in
\mathopen{\upharpoonleft}C\mathclose{\upharpoonright}
are homotopic, thus bringing a geometric justification for our
axiomatic definition of
\Pi_{2}(C)
and
\vec{\Pi}_{2}(C)
.

3.
A (geo)metric approach to the cube property

The cube condition which is at the heart of the developments in previous
sections is quite reminiscent of Gromov’s condition for characterizing
non-positively curved cubical complexes
[
gromov1987hyperbolic
]
. In order to
make the connection precise, one has to associate to every precubical set a
geodesic metric space (which is a cubical complex). Such a construction could be
performed abstractly as a geometric realization in the category of metric spaces
if this category were cocomplete… which is not the case. This leads us to
investigate a generalization of the notion of metric spaces (called

generalized metric spaces

[
grandis2009directed
]
or
Lawvere
metric spaces

[
lawvere1973metric
]
or
hemi-metric
spaces

[
goubault2013non
]
) which form a cocomplete
category. Interestingly, these spaces also allow one to encode a notion of
“time direction” in the metric, as first noticed by
Grandis
[
grandis2007fundamental
,
grandis2009directed
]
, thus enabling one to
formulate a metric semantics for concurrent programs, which is not only able to
encode the direction of time but also the duration of the elapsed time during an
execution.
The use of non-positive curvature has been used by Ghrist in order to study
configuration spaces and cubical complexes
[
Ghrist1
,
ghristgeometric
,
Ghrist2
]
.

In Section
3.1
, we recall the definition of generalized metric spaces
as well as associated basic definitions and properties: the properties of the
resulting category, the symmetric variant of generalized metric spaces (which
can be thought of as being “undirected”), the topology induced by a metric,
and the various notions of paths. In Section
, we define the
realization of a precubical set as a metric space and show that the underlying
topology of the realization induced by the metric is the expected one: this
means that considering the metric bring more (as opposed to different)
information compared to the usual case. Finally, in Section
, we
recall the original definition of space with non-positive curvature, and show
that our definition corresponds with this one, through geometric realization.

The two main results are those in the end of Sections
and

(compatibility of the metric with usual topology and
correspondence of our axioms with the usual ones). We do not claim much
originality in this section, but we did our best to collect in a concise way
results which are disseminated in the literature or, worse, considered as
folklore. The reason why we have presented this here is to explain the
inspiration for our axiomatics on precubical sets, but none of the results in
this paper depend on the geometric interpretation of those axioms.

3.1.
Generalized metric spaces

Recall that a
metric space

(X,d)
consists of a set
X
equipped with a
metric
d:X\times X\to[0,\infty]
, i.e. a function such that, given
x,y,z\in X
,
we have

\begin{array}[]{rrl}(1)&\text{point equality:}&d(x,x)=0\\
(2)&\text{triangle inequality:}&d(x,z)\leqslant d(x,y)+d(y,z)\\
(3)&\text{finite distances:}&d(x,y)<\infty\\
(4)&\text{separation:}&\text{$d(x,y)=d(y,x)=0$ implies $x=y$}\\
(5)&\text{symmetry:}&d(x,y)=d(y,x)\\
\end{array}

Generalized metric space are defined similarly, but only keeping the two first
conditions:

{defi}

A
generalized metric space

(X,d)
consists of a set
X
equipped
with a function
d:X\times X\to[0,\infty]
, called generalized
metric

or
distance
, such that, given
x,y,z\in X
, we have

\begin{array}[]{rrl}(1)&\text{point equality:}&d(x,x)=0\\
(2)&\text{triangle inequality:}&d(x,z)\leqslant d(x,y)+d(y,z)\\
\end{array}

A morphism
f:(X,d_{X})\to(Y,d_{Y})
between two such spaces is a function

f:X\to Y
which is
nonexpansive
(does not increase distance): for
every
x,y\in X
,
d_{Y}(f(x),f(y))\leqslant d_{X}(x,y)
. We write
orthe\emph{categoryofgeneralizedmetricspaces}andtheirmorphisms.\par\noindent Bya\emph{space}wewillalwaysmeananobjectinthiscategory,unlessweexplicitlystatethatthespaceis``non-generalized^{\prime\prime}.ItwasobservedbyLawvere\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{lawvere1973metric}{}{}]}thatitcanalternativelybedefinedasthecategoryofcategoriesandfunctorsenrichedovertheposetalcategory
V
=[0,∞]
(withanarrow
x→y
whenever
x⩾y
)equippedwiththemonoidalstructureinducedbyaddition.Ourmaininterestinconsideringthiscategoryinsteadofthefullsubcategoryof(usual)metricspacesisdouble.Firstly,removingconditions(3)and(4)makesthecategorymuchmorewell-behaved(thecategoryofgeneralizedmetricspaceshassmallcolimits,whichisnotthecaseformetricspaces).Ofcourse,wegenerallyworkwithseparated(
T_0
)spacesandwewillhavetoshowthatcolimitsweobtainfromsuchspacesarestillseparated.Secondly,removingcondition(5)allowsustoencodeanotionofdirectionofthespace:intuitively,apoint\penalty\
x
isreally``before^{\prime\prime}apoint\penalty\
y
wheneverthedistance
d(x,y)
isfiniteand
d(y,x)=∞
.\par\exa Given$a,b\in\mathbb{R}$,wewrite$[a,b]$fortheintervalequippedwiththeusualmetricgivenby$d(x,y)=|y-x|$.Wewrite$\overrightarrow{[a,b]}$forthesameintervalmetrizedby$$d(x,y)\quad=\quad\begin{cases}y-x&\text{if $y\geqslant x$}\\
\infty&\text{if $y<x$}\end{cases}$$Inparticular,weoftenwrite$I$(resp.\ $\vec{I}$)insteadof$[0,1]$(resp.\ $\overrightarrow{[0,1]}$)forthe(\emph{directed})\emph{standardinterval}.Similarly,wewrite$\mathbb{R}$(resp.\ $\vec{\mathbb{R}}$)forthe(\emph{directed})\emph{realline},whichisalsosometimescalledthe\emph{Sorgenfreyline}\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{sorgenfrey1947topological,goubault2013non}{}{}]}.\par\exa The\emph{directedunitcircle}$\vec{\mathbb{S}}^{1}$isthesetofcomplexpointsoftheform$\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}2\pi\theta}$,with$\theta\in\mathbb{R}$,equippedwiththedistance$d(x,y)=\bigwedge\left\{\rho-\theta\ \middle|\ x=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}2\pi\theta},y=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}2\pi\rho},\rho\geqslant\theta\right\}$.\par\defi An\emph{isometry}$f:X\to Y$isadistance-preservingmorphism,i.e.\ satisfies$d_{Y}(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))=d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})$forevery$x,x^{\prime}\in X$.\par\begin{rem}Isomorphisms in\penalty\ $reisometries.\end{rem}\par\begin{lem}Suppose given an isometry $f:X\to Y$. For every $x,y\in X$, we have
$f(x)=f(y)$ implies $d(x,y)=0=d(y,x)$. In particular, when $X$ separated $f$
is injective, and we write $f^{-1}(y)$ for the unique antecedent of $y\in Y$
under\penalty\ $f$.
\end{lem}\par\noindent Wewrite\penalty\ $d_0$fortheconstantdistanceequalto\penalty\ $0$and$d_\infty$forthedistancesuchthat$d(x,x)=0$and\penalty\ $d(x,y)=\infty$for$x\neq y$.\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Limits and colimits}
We now show that the category of generalized metric spaces has all limits and
colimits and describe how to explicitly construct those. These are also studied
in\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{grandis2009directed, goubault2013non}{}{}]}.
\par\begin{prop}The category $scompleteandcocomplete.Moreover,theforgetfulfunctor$$admitsbothaleftandarightadjointandthuspreserveslimitsandcolimits.\end{prop}\begin{proof}Since the category\penalty\ $\mathcal{V}$ is complete and cocomplete, with products (resp.\ coproducts) being given by supremum (resp.\ infimum), by general results about
enriched categories\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{betti1983variation}{}{}]}, the category
$\mathcal{V}$ has small limits and colimits. The left (resp.\ right)
adjoint to the forgetful functor $$ is the functor which to a
set\penalty\ $X$ associates the generalized metric space $(X,d_{0})$ (resp.\ $(X,d_{\infty})$). The underlying set of a limit of metric spaces is thus the
limit of the underlying sets and similarly for colimits.
\end{proof}\par Limitsandcolimitscanbeconstructedexplicitlyasfollows.Itiswell-knownthatitisenoughtoconstructall(co)productsand(co)equalizerstoconstructall(co)limits.\par\begin{lem}Given a family $(X_{i},d_{i})_{i\in I}$ of metric spaces, its product (resp.\ coproduct) is the set $\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}$ (resp.\ $\coprod_{i\in I}X_{i}$),
respectively equipped with the distances
$$d_{\prod X_{i}}((x_{i}),(y_{i}))=\bigvee_{i\in I}d_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\qquad\qquad d_{\coprod X_{i}}(x,y)=\begin{cases}d_{i}(x,y)&\text{if $x,y\in x_{i}$}\\
\infty&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}$$\end{lem}\par\par\begin{lem}Given a pair of morphisms $f,g:(X,d_{X})\to(Y,d_{Y})$, their equalizer is the
equalizer set $Z=\left\{x\in X\ \middle|\ f(x)=g(x)\right\}$ equipped with the restriction of
$d_{X}$ as distance.
\end{lem}\par\begin{lem}Given a pair of morphisms $f,g:(X,d_{X})\to(Y,d_{Y})$, the coequalizer of\penalty\ $f$
and\penalty\ $g$ is the set $Y/{\approx}$, where\penalty\ $\approx$ is the smallest equivalence
relation such that $y\approx y^{\prime}$ whenever there exists $x\in X$ with $y=f(x)$
and $y^{\prime}=g(x)$, equipped with the following distance. Given two points
$x,y\in Y$, a ``chain'' from $x$ to $y$
is a sequence of points $x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2},\ldots,x_{n},y_{n}\in Y$ such that
$x=x_{1}$, $y_{i}\approx x_{i+1}$, and $y_{n}=y$. The length of such a chain\penalty\ $u$ is
defined to be $l(u)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(x_{i},y_{i})$. The distance between two points
$x,y\in Y/{\approx}$ is the infimum of the lengths of chains from a
representative of\penalty\ $x$ to a representative of\penalty\ $y$ (in fact, it does not depend
on the choice of representatives for\penalty\ $x$ and\penalty\ $y$).
\end{lem}\par\begin{rem}With the notations of previous lemma, given $x,y\in Y$, the sequence $x,y$ is
a chain from\penalty\ $x$ to\penalty\ $y$ and thus $d_{Y/{\approx}}(x,y)\leqslant d_{Y}(x,y)$.
\end{rem}\par\exa Thedirectedcircle(seeExample\penalty\ \ref{ex:dcircle})canbeconstructedfrom\penalty\ $\overrightarrow{[0,1]}$asacolimit,byidentifyingthepoints\penalty\ $0$and\penalty\ $1$.\par\exa Considerthespaces$(I_{n},d_{n})$indexedby$n\in\mathbb{N}$with$I_{n}=[0,1]$anddistance$d_{n}(x,y)=|y-x|/n$.Thecolimit$I_{\omega}=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}/{\approx}$where$\approx$identifiespoints$0$,resp.\ $1$,invarious$I_{n}$isthecolimitofthesetsequippedwiththedistance\penalty\ $d_{\omega}$suchthatgiven$x\in I_{n}$and$y\in I_{m}$,$$d_{\omega}(x,y)\quad=\quad\begin{cases}0&\text{if $x,y\in\left\{0,1\right\}$}\\
|y-x|/n&\text{if $n=m$}\\
(x/n+y/m)\vee((1-x)/n+(1-y)/m)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}$$Inparticular,wehave$d_{\omega}(0,1)=0$,whichshowsthatcoequalizersofseparatedspacesarenotnecessarilyseparated.Noticethat,inthespace$I_{\omega}$,ifwe``cutinthemiddle^{\prime\prime}alltheintervalsweobtaintwo``star-shaped^{\prime\prime}spaceswhicharebothseparated.Thespace$I_{\omega}$canthenbeobtainedasapushoutofthetwospaces(overthediscretespacewith$\mathbb{N}$aspoints),showingthatseparatedspacesarenoteitherclosedunderpushouts.\par\begin{rem}Since the category\penalty\ $scocomplete,wecaneasilymimicthedefinitionsofSection\penalty\ \ref{sec:pcs-sem}inordertoassociateageneralizedmetricspacetoeachprogram.Wedonotdetailthishere,becausewewillbeabletoreusetheprecubicalsemanticsandrealizeitasaspace,seeSection\penalty\ \ref{sec:pcs-greal},insteadofstartingalloveragain.Inparticular,anactioninthissemanticswilltypicallyberealizedasadirectedunitinterval\penalty\ $\vec{I}$(seeExample\penalty\ \ref{ex:dui}):thiscorrespondstotheintuitionthatexecutinganactionisadirectedprocessandtakes\T1\guillemetleft\penalty\ oneunitoftime\penalty\ \T1\guillemetright(butofcourse,otherdurationchoicescouldbemadedependingonthenatureoftheactions,asillustratedinnextremark).Moreover,parallelcompositionisnaturallyinterpretedasproductofmetricspaces:thetimetakentoexecutetwoprogramsinparallelisthemaximumofbothexecutiontimes.\end{rem}\par\begin{rem}In order to illustrate why removing the separation axiom makes sense in this
context, consider the action $\mathsf{nop}$, which is instantaneously executed and

\end{rem}\par Becauseourspacesarenotsupposedtobeseparated,itwillsometimesbeusefultoconsiderthefollowingkindofquotient.\par\defi Givenaspace\penalty\ $X$,anequivalencerelation\penalty\ $\approx$on\penalty\ $X$iscalled\emph{instantaneous}when$x\approx y$implies$d(x,y)=0$forevery$x,y\in X$.\par\begin{lem}Given a space\penalty\ $X$ and a instantaneous equivalence relation\penalty\ $\approx$, the
quotient morphism $X\to X/{\approx}$ is an isometry.
\end{lem}\begin{proof}The quotient space\penalty\ $X/{\approx}$ can be computed as the coequalizer of
${\approx}\rightrightarrows X$, where the two arrows are the projections of
the relation ${\approx}\subseteq X\times X$. Given $x,y\in X$ and a chain
$x_{0},y_{0},\ldots,x_{n},y_{n}$ with $x_{0}=x$, $y_{n}=y$, by the triangle inequality we
have
\@@amsalign
and therefore $d_{X}(x,y)\leqslant d_{X/{\approx}}(x,y)$. Conversely, since $x,y$ is
a chain, we have that $d_{X/{\approx}}(x,y)\leqslant d_{X}(x,y)$.
\end{proof}\par\noindent Inparticular,givenageneralizedmetricspace\penalty\ $X$,thefreeseparatedspacecanbeconstructedas\penalty\ $X/{\approx}$,where$\approx$isthesmallestequivalencerelationsuchthat$d(x,y)=0$or$d(y,x)=0$implies$x\approx y$,for$x,y\in X$.Thequotientmapwillbeanisometrywhentherelation\penalty\ $\approx$isinstantaneous,i.e.\ when$d(x,y)=0$implies$d(y,x)=0$.Thecolimitsofnon-generalizedmetricspacesusuallyconsideredinliteraturecanbeobtainedfromthosedescribedherebyfreelyseparatingtheresultingcolimits.\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Symmetric metric spaces}
The category of spaces which are symmetric forms an interesting subcategory, in
which most of the usual properties of (non-generalized) metric spaces are still
valid. They are sometimes also called \emph{pseudo-metric spaces} in the
literature\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{wilson1931quasi, albert1941note, kelly1963bitopological}{}{}]}.
\par\defi We write $orthefullsubcategoryof\penalty\ $hose object are
symmetric generalized metric spaces, i.e.\ spaces $(X,d)$ such that
$d(x,y)=d(y,x)$ for every\penalty\ $x,y\in X$.
\par\noindent The constructions of Proposition\penalty\ \ref{prop:gmet-limits} are easily checked to
preserve the symmetry of spaces:
\par\begin{lem}The category $scompleteandcocomplete.\end{lem}\par\begin{rem}Notice that given a symmetric space $(X,d)$ and $x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}\in X$, we have
$$|d(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})-d(x,y)|\quad\leqslant\quad|d(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})-d(x,y^{\prime})|+|d(x,y^{\prime})-d(x,y)|\quad\leqslant\quad d(x,x^{\prime})\vee d(y,y^{\prime})$$the last step using the well-known ``reverse triangle inequality''. The
distance\penalty\ $d$ can thus be seen as a morphism $d:X\times X\to[0,\infty]$ in
$\end{rem}\par\noindent Anygeneralizedmetricspacecancanonicallybesymmetrizedintwowaysasfollows.\par\begin{prop}The forgetful functor $$ admits a left adjoint sending a space
$(X,d)$ to the symmetric space $(X,\overline{d})$ where the metric\penalty\ $\overline{d}$ is
called the \emph{symmetric metric} generated by\penalty\ $d$ and is defined by
$$\overline{d}(x,y)\quad=\quad\bigwedge_{x=x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{2n},x_{2n+1}=y}\sum_{i=0}^{n}d(x_{i+1},x_{i})+d(x_{i+1},x_{i+2})$$It also admits a right adjoint sending $(X,d)$ to the space $(X,d^{\vee})$
equipped with the distance defined by $d^{\vee}(x,y)=d(x,y)\vee d(y,x)$. The
forgetful functor thus preserves limits and colimits.
\end{prop}\par\exa Forinstance,considerthedirectedplane$\vec{\mathbb{R}}^{2}$,whosedistanceisgivenby$d((x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2}))=d_{\vec{\mathbb{R}}}(x_{1},y_{1})\vee d_{\vec{\mathbb{R}}}(x_{2},y_{2})$,and$d_{\vec{\mathbb{R}}}$isdescribedinExample\penalty\ \ref{ex:dr}.Theleftandrightadjointofthepropositionrespectivelyequip$\mathbb{R}^{2}$withthedistances\penalty\ $\overline{d}$and$d^{\vee}$suchthat$$\overline{d}((x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2}))\quad=\quad\begin{cases}|y_{1}-x_{1}|\vee|y_{2}-x_{2}|&\text{if $(y_{1}-x_{1})(y_{2}-x_{2})\geqslant 0$}\\
|y_{1}-x_{1}|+|y_{2}-x_{2}|&\text{if $(y_{1}-x_{1})(y_{2}-x_{2})\leqslant 0$}\\
\end{cases}$$and$$d^{\vee}((x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2}))\quad=\quad\begin{cases}|y_{1}-x_{1}|\vee|y_{2}-x_{2}|&\text{if $(y_{1}-x_{1})(y_{2}-x_{2})\geqslant 0$}\\
\infty&\text{if $(y_{1}-x_{1})(y_{2}-x_{2})\leqslant 0$}\\
\end{cases}$$\par\begin{rem}For every points $x$ and $y$ in a generalized metric space $(X,d)$, we have
$d(x,y)\geqslant\overline{d}(x,y)$ (as witnessed by the unit of the monad on\penalty\ $nducedbytheadjunction).Moreover,if$d$isasymmetricmetricthen$\lx@text@overline{d}=d$(thecomonadon\penalty\ $nduced by the adjunction is the identity comonad).
\end{rem}\par\begin{rem}We say a \emph{self-dual} category\penalty\ $\mathcal{C}$ is a category equipped with an
isomorphism $\mathcal{C}\cong\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}$ which is the identity on objects. The above
construction for the left adjoint functor is a particular case of the
construction of the free self-dual $\mathcal{V}$-category on a $\mathcal{V}$-category, and a
variant of the more well-known construction of the enveloping $\mathcal{V}$-groupoid
of a $\mathcal{V}$-category.
\end{rem}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{The underlying topological space of a metric space}
\par We are now interested in equipping our spaces with a topology, i.e.\ in
constructing a decent functor $$ (in particular, we want directed
paths, see Definition\penalty\ \ref{def:path}, to be continuous). In the case of
symmetric spaces, a satisfactory answer is provided by the usual functor
$$ sending a symmetric space $(X,d)$ to the topological space\penalty\ $X$
equipped with the metric topology, which is generated by open balls
$$B^{\varepsilon}(x)\quad=\quad\left\{y\in X\ \middle|\ d(x,y)<\varepsilon\right\}$$for $x\in X$ and $\varepsilon>0$. In
particular, by Remark\penalty\ \ref{rem:d-morphism}, given a space $(X,d)$, the
distance $\overline{d}:X\times X\to X$ is continuous.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$when\penalty\ $y$ ``moves'' vertically to\penalty\ $y^{\prime}$, the value of $d(x,y)$ suddenly
``jumps'' from a finite value to $\infty$. Similarly in $\vec{\mathbb{S}}^{1}$ (figure on
the right), when\penalty\ $y$ moves to\penalty\ $y^{\prime}$, the value of $d(x,y)$ jumps from\penalty\ $0$ to
$2\pi$.
\end{rem}\par\begin{prop}The functor $$ preserves finite limits and small
coproducts.
\end{prop}\begin{proof}It can be directly checked that the functor preserves binary products,
equalizers, and small coproducts, as described in Section\penalty\ \ref{sec:limits},
see also\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{goubault2013non}{}{}]}.
\end{proof}\par\begin{rem}The functor does not preserve coequalizers.
Namely, if we consider the colimit of Example\penalty\ \ref{ex:In}, the colimit as
topological spaces has points $0$ and $1$ separated in\penalty\ $I_{\omega}$, whereas
$d_{\omega}(0,1)=0$ and thus the points are not separated in the topological
space associated to the colimit of generalized metric spaces.
\end{rem}\par\begin{rem}The functor does not preserve infinite products. Namely, given a
family\penalty\ $(X_{i})$ of generalized metric spaces, the underlying topology of the
generalized metric space $\prod_{i}X_{i}$ is the box topology, which is finer than
the product topology.
\end{rem}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Directed paths}
In this section, we define the notion of directed path in a generalized metric
space.
\par\defi A \emph{path} in a space\penalty\ $X$ is a continuous function $\gamma:I\to X$. Notice
that\penalty\ $\gamma$ is not required to be nonexpansive, i.e.\ it is a path in the
underlying topological space. The point $x=\gamma(0)$ (resp.\ $y=\gamma(1)$) is
called the \emph{source} (resp.\ \emph{target}) of the path. We often write
$\gamma:x\twoheadrightarrow y$ to indicate that $\gamma$ is a path from\penalty\ $x$ to\penalty\ $y$.
The \emph{length}\penalty\ $\|\gamma\|$ of a path\penalty\ $\gamma$ is defined by
$$\|\gamma\|\quad=\quad\bigvee_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\bigvee_{0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{n}=1}\sum_{i}d(\gamma(t_{i}),\gamma(t_{i+1}))$$A path\penalty\ $\gamma$ is called \emph{directed} (or a \emph{dipath}, or a
\emph{rectifiable} path) when its length is finite.
\par\exa In the directed circle $\vec{\mathbb{S}}^{1}$ (see Example\penalty\ \ref{ex:dcircle}), directed
paths are those which are ``turning counter-clockwise'', i.e.\ maps of the form
$t\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta(t)}$ where $\theta:I\to\penalty\ [0,2\pi]$ is increasing
modulo\penalty\ $2\pi$.
\par\begin{rem}By the triangle inequality, we always have
$\|\gamma\|\geqslant d(\gamma(0),\gamma(1))$ for an arbitrary path\penalty\ $\gamma$.
\end{rem}\par\par\begin{rem}By Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:gmet-mor-cont}, given a path $\gamma:I\to X$ and a morphism
$f:X\to Y$, the morphism $f\circ\gamma:I\to Y$ is also a path. Since $f$ is
nonexpansive, the path $f\circ\gamma$ is directed when\penalty\ $\gamma$ is: morphisms
preserve the direction of paths.
\end{rem}\par\par\begin{rem}Since a (non-generalized) metric space is symmetric, it does not contain any
information about a ``direction of time'' and it is thus natural to expect
that every path is directed in this case. However, this is not the case
because it is well known that, in general, a path is not necessarily
rectifiable (for instance the well-known topologist's sine curve in\penalty\ $\mathbb{R}^{2}$
defined by $\gamma(0)=0$ and $\gamma(t)=t\sin(1/t)$ for $0<t\leqslant 1$). The
definition of directed path given in Definition\penalty\ \ref{def:path} is thus not
completely satisfactory yet, and we leave the investigation of a more general
notion for future works; we expect that in the case of geometric semantics of
concurrent programs, the current and the right notions of directed paths
coincide.
Notice that many simple generalizations of the notion of directedness that one
could think of in order to overcome this problem do not work. For instance,
one could declare that a path\penalty\ $\gamma$ is directed if we have
$d(\gamma(t_{1}),\gamma(t_{2}))<\infty$, for every $t_{1},t_{2}\in I$ with
$t_{1}<t_{2}$. However, with this definition, every path of the directed unit
circle (see Example\penalty\ \ref{ex:dcircle}) would be directed.
\end{rem}\par\noindent Of course, the usual notions of homotopy and dihomotopy directly extend to our
setting:
\par\defi A \emph{homotopy} between two paths $\gamma,\rho:I\to X$ is a continuous
function $h:I\times I\to X$ such that $h(0,-)=\gamma$ and $h(1,-)=\rho$. Such
a homotopy is a \emph{dihomotopy} when $h(t,-)$ is a directed path for every
$t\in I$.
\par Intuitively, a morphism $\overrightarrow{[0,a]}\to X$ is the same as a rectifiable
path in\penalty\ $X$. However, because the maps take distance in account (they are
nonexpansive), this is only true up to an expected equivalence relation on paths:
if\penalty\ $a$ is too small, there is no possible parametrisation of the path.
A \emph{partial reparametrization} is a continuous non-decreasing map
$\theta:I\to I$ and a \emph{reparametrization} is a surjective partial
reparametrization. A \emph{trace} is an equivalence class of paths under the
relation identifying two paths\penalty\ $f$ and\penalty\ $g$ whenever there exists a
reparametrization\penalty\ $\theta$ such that $g=f\circ\theta$. It can easily be shown
that length is well-defined on traces and a trace is \emph{rectifiable} when its
length is finite. The following proposition shows that in every trace containing
a rectifiable path, there is a canonical one which corresponds to a morphism
in\penalty\ $Itsproofisadirectgeneralizationoftheoneintheclassicalcase\penalty\ \hbox{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bridson2009metric}{}{}, Prop.\penalty\ I.1.20]}}.Given$t,t'\in\mathbb{R}$with$t<t'$,wewrite$\iota_{[t,t']}:I\to[t,t']$forthefunctionsuchthat$\iota_{[t,t']}(u)=t+(t'-t)u$.\par\begin{lem}Suppose that\penalty\ $(X,d)$ is a separated space and $\gamma:I\to X$ a directed path
of length\penalty\ $a=\|\gamma\|$. The function $\lambda:I\to[0,a]$ such that
$\lambda(t)=\|\gamma\circ\iota_{[0,t]}\|$ is well-defined, continuous and
non-decreasing, and there exists a unique morphism $\tilde{\gamma}:[0,a]\to X$
such that $\tilde{\gamma}\circ\lambda=\gamma$ and
$\|\tilde{\gamma}\circ\iota_{[0,t]}\|=t$ for every $t\in[0,a]$.
\end{lem}\par\begin{rem}In order to see why we need the separation hypothesis, consider a path in a
space\penalty\ $X$ equipped with the constant distance\penalty\ $d_{0}$ equal to\penalty\ $0$. Any path
in\penalty\ $X$ has length\penalty\ $0$ and the lemma is clearly wrong.
\end{rem}\par\noindent Asadirectcorollaryofpreviouslemma,wehave:\par\begin{prop}Given a separated space\penalty\ $X$, rectifiable traces are in bijection with maps
$\overrightarrow{[0,a]}\to X$ sending distance to length, with $a\geqslant 0$.
\end{prop}\par Finally,webrieflymentionherethatwecanrecovertraditionalnotionsofdirectedspacesfromgeneralizedmetricspaces.Thenotionofd-spacehasemergedasthe``standard^{\prime\prime}modelfortopologicalspacesequippedwithanotionoftimedirection\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{grandis2009directed}{}{}]}.Itconsistsinaspacetogetherwiththespecificationofwhichpathsaretobeconsideredasdirected.\par\defi A\emph{d-space}$(X,dX)$consistsofatopologicalspace\penalty\ $X$togetherwithaset\penalty\ $dX\subseteq X^{I}$ofpathsin\penalty\ $X$,whoseelementsarecalled\emph{d-paths},whichcontainsallconstantpathsandisclosedunderconcatenationandreparametrization.Wewrite$orthecategoryofd-spaceswithcontinuousfunctionspreservingd-pathsasmorphisms.\par\begin{prop}The operation which to a metric space associates its underlying topological
space together with the set of directed paths defines a functor
$$.
\end{prop}\par\noindent Ametricspaceis\emph{acyclic}wheneverypath$f:x\twoheadrightarrow x$withthesamesourceandtargetisconstant.Tosuchaspace,wecanassociatethefollowing.\par\defi A\emph{pospace}$(X,\leqslant)$consistsofatopologicalspace\penalty\ $X$equippedwithapartialorder\penalty\ $\leqslant$.Wewrite$orthecategoryofpospaceandnon-decreasingcontinuousmaps.\par\begin{rem}Some authors impose additional restrictions on pospaces such as the fact
that\penalty\ $\leqslant$ is a closed subset of\penalty\ $X\times X$, or at least that the limit of
an increasing sequence of points is its supremum.
\end{rem}\par\begin{prop}The operation which to a metric space\penalty\ $(X,d)$ associates its underlying
topological space\penalty\ $X$, equipped with the partial order\penalty\ $\leqslant$ such that
$x\leqslant y$ if and only if $d(x,y)<\infty$ for every $x,y\in X$, extends to a
functor from the category of acyclic generalized metric spaces to the category
of pospaces.
\end{prop}\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Geodesic and length spaces}
In this section, we briefly turn our attention to length spaces, since all
geodesic spaces are such, and the CAT(0) condition we are interested in is
formulated on such spaces, by considering the ``size'' of triangles whose sides
are geodesics.
\par\defi Given a space $(X,d)$, the \emph{intrinsic metric} $d_{I}$ is defined by
$$d_{I}(x,y)\quad=\quad\bigwedge_{\gamma:x\twoheadrightarrow y}\|\gamma\|$$A space equipped with its intrinsic metric is called a \emph{length space}.
\par\exa Consider the circle
$\mathbb{S}^{1}=\left\{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}2\pi\theta}\ \middle|\ \theta\in\mathbb{R}\right\}\subseteq\mathbb{C}$ equipped
with the distance induced by the euclidian distance on\penalty\ $\mathbb{C}$, i.e.\ $d(x+\mathrm{i}y,x^{\prime}+\mathrm{i}y^{\prime})=\sqrt{(x^{\prime}-x)^{2}+(y^{\prime}-y)^{2}}$. This space is not a length
space. The associated intrinsic metric is
$$d_{I}(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta},\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho})\quad=\quad\bigwedge\left\{\rho^{\prime}-\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}-\rho^{\prime}\ \middle|\ \theta^{\prime}=\theta,\rho^{\prime}=\rho\mod 2\pi\right\}$$\par\par\defi A directed path $\gamma:I\to X$ is a \emph{geodesic} when for every $t<t^{\prime}$
in\penalty\ $I$ we have $d(\gamma(t),\gamma(t^{\prime}))=\lambda(t^{\prime}-t)$ with
$\lambda=d(\gamma(0),\gamma(1))$. A space is \emph{geodesic} (resp.\ \emph{uniquely geodesic}) when between any two points at finite distance there
exists a geodesic (resp.\ a unique geodesic).
\par\par\begin{rem}Every geodesic space is a length space, but the converse is not true in
general (for instance, the Hopf-Rinow theorem\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{hopf-rinow}{}{}]} provides
sufficient conditions on spaces so that a length space satisfying those
conditions is geodesic).
\end{rem}\par\exa The space $\mathbb{R}^{2}$ equipped with the euclidian distance is geodesic. The
subspace $\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\left\{(0,0)\right\}$ is a length space but is not geodesic
since there is no path of length\penalty\ $2=d(x,y)$ from $x=(0,-1)$ to $y=(0,1)$
(however, there exists a path of length $2+\varepsilon$ from $x$ to $y$ for
arbitrarily small $\varepsilon>0$).
\par\noindent In the case of a geodesic space, Proposition\penalty\ \ref{prop:traces-maps} can be
reformulated as follows.
\par\begin{prop}Given a separated space\penalty\ $X$, geodesic paths are in bijection with isometries
$\overrightarrow{[0,a]}\to X$, with $a\geqslant 0$.
\end{prop}\par\par\begin{lem}The subcategory of length spaces is closed under colimits.
\end{lem}\begin{proof}See\penalty\ \hbox{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bridson2009metric}{}{}, Lem.\penalty\ I.5.20]}}. It can be checked directly that the property of
being a length space is preserved under taking coproducts and coequalizers.
\end{proof}\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Geometric realization of precubical sets}
We now consider two ways of associating a generalized metric space to a
precubical set, and study the properties of the resulting space. Since here we
are mainly concerned about precubical sets arising as the cubical semantics of
concurrent programs, we study mainly finite geometric precubical sets, but try
to provide more general hypothesis when these generalizations are easy to
make. Part of the current section is a reformulation -- and generalization -- in
the categorical language of properties studied in the framework of polyhedral
complexes\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bridson2009metric}{}{}]}. The two main results of this section are
that metric and topological geometric realization provide comparable
constructions (Theorem\penalty\ \ref{thm:geom-real-gmet-top}) and that realization of
{npc} precubical sets are CAT(0) cubical complexes (Theorem\penalty\ \ref{thm:pcs-cat0}).
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Geometric realization}
\par Recall from Example\penalty\ \ref{ex:ui} that we write $I=[0,1]$ for the \emph{standard
interval}, and we write\penalty\ $I^{n}$ for the \emph{standard (topological) $n$-cube}
obtained as the product of $n$ copies of $I$. We write $\delta^{-}=0$ and
$\delta^{+}=1$. Given $i$ with $0\leqslant i<n$, and $\epsilon=-$ (resp.\ $\epsilon=+$),
the set of points in $I^{n}$ whose $i$-th coordinate is $\delta^{\epsilon}$ is
isomorphic to $I^{n-1}$ and called the $i$-th back (resp.\ front) \emph{face} of
the cube. Now, consider the functor $I:\square\to uchthattheimageofanobject$n\in\square$is$I^n$,andtheimagesofmorphisms\penalty\ $\varepsilon^\epsilon_{i,n}$arethemorphisms$I(\varepsilon^\epsilon_{i,n}):I^{n}\to I^{n+1}$suchthat$$I(\varepsilon^{\epsilon}_{i,n})(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1})\quad=\quad(x_{0},\ldots,x_{i-1},\delta^{\epsilon},x_{i},\ldots,x_{n})$$i.e.\ theyaretheinclusionsofthefacesofacubeintothecube.Atopologicalspacecanbeobtainedfromaprecubicalset\penalty\ $C$bytakingatopological$n$-cubeforeachelementof$C(n)$angluingthemaccordingtofaces.\par\defi The\textbf{geometric realization}functor$\left|-\right|:\hat{\square}\to sthefunctorobtainedastheleftKanextensionof$I:\square\to longtheYonedaembedding$y:\square\to\hat{\square}$.Moreexplicitly,givenacubicalset$C$,itsgeometricrealizationisthespace$$\left|C\right|\quad=\quad\left(\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}I^{n}\times C_{n}\right)/\approx$$where$\approx$isthesmallestequivalencerelationsuchthat$(x,c)\approx(y,d)$whenever$d=\partial^{\epsilon}_{i,n}(c)$forsomeindices$\epsilon,i,n$and$I(\varepsilon^{\epsilon}_{i,n})(y)=x$.\par\begin{rem}One can define similarly a functor $I:\square\to bypost-composingpreviousfunctorwiththeforgetfulfunctor$$),whichinduces,byleftKanextensionalongtheYonedaembedding$y:\square\to\hat{\square}$,a\textbf{topological geometric realization}functor$\left|-\right|:\hat{\square}\to Unless we add the adjective ``topological'',
``geometric realization'' will always be meant in generalized metric spaces.
\end{rem}\par\noindent ByProposition\penalty\ \ref{prop:free-s-metric},theforgetfulfunctor$$preservescolimits,andthereforegeometricrealizationcommuteswithit.Andsimilarly,itcommuteswiththesymmetrizationfunctor$$,whichisaleftadjoint.Inthefollowing,wewillmainlyfocusonsymmetricspacesforsimplicity.\par Forevery$n$-cube$c\in C(n)$thereisacanonicalmorphismofmetricspaces$\iota_c:I^n\to\left|C\right|$suchthattheimageof$x\in 7.8]}},givenapoint$x\in\left|C\right|$andan$n$-cube$c\in C(n)$suchthat$x$occursintheimageof$\iota_c$,wewrite$$\varepsilon(x,c)\quad=\quad\bigwedge\left\{d_{I^{n}}(\iota^{-1}_{c}(x),K)\ \middle|\ \middle|\ \text{$n\in\mathbb{N}$ and $c\in C(n)$ is such that $x$ belongs to the image of $\iota_{c}$}\right\}$$Itiseasytoshowthat$\varepsilon(x)>0$foranypoint\penalty\ $x\in\left|C\right|$,see\penalty\ \hbox{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bridson2009metric}{}{}, 7.33]}}.Thisconstant,calledthe\emph{escapedistance}from\penalty\ $x$,intuitivelyreflectsthefactthatapath(orachain)startingfrom\penalty\ $x$andgoingoutsideasimplexcontaining\penalty\ $x$willatleastbeoflength$\varepsilon(x)$.Otherwisesaid,thedistancefrom\penalty\ $x$toapoint\penalty\ $y$``near\penalty\ $x${}^{\prime\prime}(i.e.\ atdistancelessthan$\varepsilon(x)$)willbethesamewhetherweconsiderthedistancewithinthefaceorinthewholecomplex:\par\begin{lem}Suppose given a precubical set\penalty\ $C$ with no self-intersection, and a point\penalty\ $y$
such that $d_{|C|}(x,y)<\varepsilon(x)$. Then any cube\penalty\ $c$ which contains\penalty\ $y$
also contains\penalty\ $x$ and we have
$d_{\left|C\right|}(x,y)=d_{I^{n}}(\iota_{c}^{-1}(x),\iota_{c}^{-1}(y))$.
\end{lem}\begin{proof}See\penalty\ \hbox{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bridson2009metric}{}{}, Lem.\penalty\ 7.9]}}. Fix a ``chain'' $u=x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}$ in
$\left|C\right|$ from $x=x_{0}$ to $y=x_{n}$, i.e.\ a sequence of points such that $x_{i}$
and $x_{i+1}$ both belong to $\iota_{c_{i}}$ for some given cube $c_{i}\in C(n_{i})$
(to be precise, the $c_{i}$ are part of the data defining the chain). Its length
is by definition
$l(u)=\sum_{i}d_{I^{n_{i}}}(\iota_{c_{i}}^{-1}(x_{i}),\iota_{c_{i}}^{-1}(x_{i+1}))$ and
the distance $d_{\left|C\right|}(x,y)$ is the infimum of lengths of such
chains. The points\penalty\ $x_{1}$ and\penalty\ $x_{2}$ belong to the image of\penalty\ $\iota_{c_{1}}$. If we
suppose moreover that $l(u)<\varepsilon(x)$ then $x$ also belongs
to\penalty\ $\iota_{c_{1}}$, and by the triangle inequality the chain
$u^{\prime}=x_{0},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,x_{n}$ has smaller length. We can conclude by a simple
induction.
\end{proof}\par\begin{lem}Given a precubical set\penalty\ $C$ with no self-intersection, its geometric
realization\penalty\ $\left|C\right|$ is separated.
\end{lem}\begin{proof}This is a corollary of Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:local-distance}, since the spaces\penalty\ $I^{n}$
are separated.
\end{proof}\par\par\begin{lem}The geometric realization of a precubical set is a length space.
\end{lem}\begin{proof}The spaces $I^{n}$ are length spaces and, by Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:length-colimit},
length spaces are closed under colimits.
\end{proof}\par\begin{lem}The geometric realization of a finite dimensional precubical set\penalty\ $C$ is a
complete space.
\end{lem}\begin{proof}The ideas is as follows, see \hbox{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bridson2009metric}{}{}, Thm.\penalty\ 7.13]}}, of which this proof is
a variant, for details. Given a Cauchy sequence $(x_{n})$ in\penalty\ $\left|C\right|$, we
show that it contains a convergent subsequence. The points\penalty\ $x_{i}$ belong to the
image of $\iota_{c_{i}}$ for some $n_{i}$-cell $c_{i}\in C(n_{i})$. Up to taking a
subsequence, we can suppose that all the $n_{i}$ are equal and we write\penalty\ $n$ for
the corresponding integer: the points\penalty\ $c_{i}$ are the images $\iota_{c_{i}}(x_{i})$
for some points $x_{i}\in I^{n}$. Up to taking a further subsequence, we can
suppose that the sequence\penalty\ $(x_{i})$ converges to a point\penalty\ $x_{\infty}$. Now, noting
that the $\iota_{c_{i}}$ are nonexpansive, using an argument based on
$\varepsilon(x_{\infty})$ as in the proof of Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:length-colimit}, one
can show that the $c_{i}$ are all equal to some\penalty\ $c_{\infty}$ for\penalty\ $i$ big enough,
and conclude that the subsequence converges to $\iota_{c_{\infty}}(x_{\infty})$
in\penalty\ $\left|C\right|$.
\end{proof}\par\par WehaveseeninLemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:local-distance}thatthegeometricrealizationofaprecubicalsetislocallyisometrictoaspace\penalty\ $I^n$.Theproofusedheredoesnotreallydependonthechoiceofthefunctor\penalty\ $I:\square\to sed to specify
the realization of representable precubical sets, and would extend to other
choices. We however conjecture that, with this specific choice, this property is
more ``global'' in the sense that the canonical inclusions of standard cubes in
the realizations are in fact isometries. The intuition behind this is that
within a cube any two points are at distance at most one, and that a non-trivial
chain going outside a cube has to cross another cube and will thus be of length
more than one.
\par\begin{conjecture}Given a finite dimensional geometric precubical set\penalty\ $C$, $n\in\mathbb{N}$ and
$c\in C(n)$ the morphism $\iota_{c}:I^{n}\to\left|C\right|$ is an isometry.
\end{conjecture}\par\begin{rem}Notice that the above conjecture is not true if the precubical set is not
supposed to be geometric. For instance, consider the following precubical
set\penalty\ $C$:

$5$,sothatthelengthofthediagonalis$\sqrt{5}>2$.\end{rem}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{The topology of the geometric realization}
In previous section, we have introduced two possible geometric realizations: in
metric spaces and in topological spaces. We now show that the two agree for the
precubical sets of interest here. Notice that, by
Remark\penalty\ \ref{rem:gmet-top-colimits}, we have no hope to show this using very
general arguments since the forgetful functor $$ does not preserve
colimits (nor even finite ones).
Consider a symmetric generalized metric space\penalty\ $(X,d)$. We write
$U:$ for the forgetful functor described in
Section\penalty\ \ref{sec:gmet-top}.
\par\begin{lem}Given an equivalence relation\penalty\ $R$ on\penalty\ $X$, the underlying sets of\penalty\ $(UX)/R$ and
$U(X/R)$ are the same and the identity function $f:(UX)/R\to U(X/R)$ is
continuous.
\end{lem}\begin{proof}The underlying sets of both\penalty\ $(UX)/R$ and\penalty\ $U(X/R)$ are the same because the
forgetful functors $$ and $$ both admit a right
adjoint (see Proposition\penalty\ \ref{prop:gmet-limits}).
Suppose given $V$ open in\penalty\ $U(X/R)$: for every $x\in V$ there exists
$\varepsilon>0$ such that $B^{\varepsilon}_{X/R}(x)\subseteq V$. Thus, for every
$x\in f^{-1}(V)$, there exists $\varepsilon>0$ such that
$f^{-1}(B^{\varepsilon}_{X/R}(f(x)))\subseteq f^{-1}(V)$. Given\penalty\ $x\in X$, we
have $B^{\varepsilon}_{X}(x)\subseteq B^{\varepsilon}_{X/R}(x)$ by
Remark\penalty\ \ref{rem:coeq-d}. Therefore, for every $x\in X$ such that
$x\in\pi^{-1}(V)$, there exists $\varepsilon>0$ such that
$B^{\varepsilon}_{X}(x)\subseteq\pi^{-1}(V)$, from which we can conclude that
$\pi^{-1}(V)$ is open.
\end{proof}\par\noindent The following lemma is well-known\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{rudin1964principles}{}{}, Thm.\penalty\ 4.17]}:
\par\begin{lem}A continuous bijection\penalty\ $f:X\to Y$ between topological spaces such that\penalty\ $X$ is
compact and\penalty\ $Y$ is separated is an homeomorphism.
\end{lem}\begin{proof}Given a closed set\penalty\ $U$ of\penalty\ $X$, $U$ is compact since\penalty\ $X$ is, therefore $f(U)$
is also compact, and therefore $f(U)$ is closed since\penalty\ $Y$ is
separated. Therefore $(f^{-1})^{-1}(U)=f(U)$ is closed and $f^{-1}$ is
continuous since the preimage of a closed set is closed.
\end{proof}\par\begin{prop}The geometric realization of finite geometric precubical sets commutes with
the forgetful functor $$.
\end{prop}\begin{proof}Given a finite geometric precubical set\penalty\ $C$, the colimit defining its
geometric realization can be computed as a quotient of the space
$X=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\coprod_{x\in C(n)}I(n)$, by a relation that we
denote\penalty\ $R$. The space\penalty\ $UX$ is compact, as a finite coproduct of compact spaces
(the functor\penalty\ $U$ commutes with coproducts, see
Proposition\penalty\ \ref{prop:gmet-top-preservation}), and thus the space $(UX)/R$ is
compact too, as a quotient of a compact space. Since\penalty\ $C$ is geometric, and
thus without self-intersection, its geometric realization\penalty\ $U(X/R)$ is
separated by Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:greal-separated}. Finally, the identity function
$(UX)/R\to U(X/R)$ is continuous by Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:gr-id-cont} and thus an
homeomorphism by Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:bij-homeo}.
\end{proof}\par\noindent Since the topology of a space is determined locally, previous proposition
can be generalized to the case of precubical sets which are only locally finite:
\par\defi A precubical set is \emph{locally finite} if for every vertex\penalty\ $x$, only
finitely many cubes have $x$ as iterated vertex.
\par\begin{thm}The geometric realization of locally finite geometric precubical sets commutes
with the forgetful functor $$.
\end{thm}\begin{proof}Suppose given a locally finite geometric precubical set\penalty\ $C$ and a
point\penalty\ $x\in\left|C\right|$. We write\penalty\ $c$ for the carrier of\penalty\ $x$, i.e.\ the cube
in\penalty\ $C$ of lowest dimension which contains\penalty\ $x$. Consider the precubical
set\penalty\ $C_{c}$defined as the smallest precubical subset of\penalty\ $C$ which contains all
the cubes having\penalty\ $c$ as iterated face (the star of\penalty\ $c$): its cubes are faces
of maximal cubes with\penalty\ $c$ as iterated face. Since\penalty\ $C$ is supposed to be both
locally finite and geometric, the precubical set\penalty\ $C_{c}$ is easily shown to be
finite and we can apply Proposition\penalty\ \ref{prop:geom-real-gmet-top}. By
Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:local-distance}, a neighborhood of\penalty\ $x$ in the geometric
realization of\penalty\ $C$ is isometric to a neighborhood of\penalty\ $x$ in the geometric
realization of\penalty\ $C_{c}$ (because the lemma shows that the distance is the
distance of standard cubes). Therefore, locally around\penalty\ $x$, the metric
topology is the colimit topology.
\end{proof}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Cubical complexes}
Geometric precubical sets give rise to cubical complexes, in the following
sense.
\par\defi A {cubical complex} $K$ is a topological space of the form
$$K\quad=\quad\left(\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}I^{n_{\lambda}}\right)/{\approx}$$where $\Lambda$ is a set, $(n_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}$ is a family of
integers, and $\approx$ is an equivalence relation, such that, writing
$p_{\lambda}:I^{n_{\lambda}}\to K$ for the restriction of the quotient map
$\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}I^{n_{\lambda}}\to K$, we have
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@for every $\lambda\in\Lambda$, the map $p_{\lambda}$ is injective,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@given $\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda$, if $p_{\lambda}(I^{n_{\lambda}})\cap p_{\mu}(I^{n_{\mu}})\neq\emptyset$ then there is an isometry from a face
$J_{\lambda}$ of\penalty\ $I^{n_{\lambda}}$ to a face $J_{\mu}$ of $I^{n_{\mu}}$ such that
$p_{\lambda}(x)=p_{\mu}(y)$ if and only if $y=h_{\lambda,\mu}(x)$.
\end{enumerate}A \emph{directed cubical complex} is defined similarly, as a d-space of the
form $K=\left(\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}I^{n_{\lambda}}\right)/{\approx}$.
\par\begin{prop}The geometric realization of a geometric precubical set is a cubical complex.
\end{prop}\par\noindent Moreover, the metric of the geometric realization of a geometric precubical set
is precisely the intrinsic metric (as defined in\penalty\ \hbox{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bridson2009metric}{}{}, Sect.\penalty\ I.7.33]}}).
\par\begin{rem}Notice that the geometric realization of precubical complexes of
Example\penalty\ \ref{ex:pcs-geom} which are not geometric are not precubical
complexes.
\end{rem}\par\par\begin{rem}The converse of the above lemma is not true. For instance, one can construct a
M\"{o}bius strip as a complex, and it cannot be obtained as the geometric
realization of a geometric precubical set because there is a ``mismatch of
direction'' of the edges. One can actually show that directed geometric
realizations of geometric precubical sets are precisely directed cubical
complexes.
\end{rem}\par\par\par\par\noindent Finally, we mention the following useful fact, whose proof can be found
in\penalty\ \hbox{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bridson2009metric}{}{}, 7.33]}}.
\par\begin{lem}A finite dimensional precubical complex is geodesic.
\end{lem}\par\noindent To sum up the above properties shown on geometric realizations of geometric
precubical sets, we have:
\par\begin{prop}Finite dimensional precubical complexes are complete geodesic length spaces.
\end{prop}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Directed geometric realization}
Interestingly, all the above properties can easily be checked to hold for a
variant of the geometric realization which is really ``directed'', and makes
much more sense from a concurrency point of view. In Definition\penalty\ \ref{def:greal}
of geometric realization, we can replace the functor $I:\square\to ending\penalty\ $n$to$I^n$bythefunctor$\vec{I}:\square\to hich send\penalty\ $n$ to
$\vec{I}^{n}$ and thus obtain a variant of the notion of geometric realization where
not every path is directed.
\par\defi The {directed geometric realization} $\mathopen{\upharpoonleft}C\mathclose{\upharpoonright}$ of a precubical complex
is the functor obtained as the left Kan extension of $\vec{I}:\square\to longtheYonedaembedding$y:\square\to\hat{\square}$.\par\begin{prop}The directed geometric realization of a finite dimensional geometric
precubical set is a complete geodesic length space.
\end{prop}\par Givenaprecubicalset\penalty\ $C$,wecanconsideritsdirectedgeometricrealization\penalty\ $\mathopen{\upharpoonleft}C\mathclose{\upharpoonright}$.Avertexin\penalty\ $C$canbeseenasamorphism$Y0\to C$(where$Y$denotestheYonedafunctor,seeSection\penalty\ \ref{sec:pcs})andthusinduces,byfunctorialityoftherealization,apoint\penalty\ $\mathopen{\upharpoonleft}x\mathclose{\upharpoonright}$of$\mathopen{\upharpoonleft}C\mathclose{\upharpoonright}$,whichisgivenbytheimageofthemorphism$\mathopen{\upharpoonleft}Y0\mathclose{\upharpoonright}\to\mathopen{\upharpoonleft}C\mathclose{\upharpoonright}$.Similarly,apath(resp.\ dipath)\penalty\ $f$in\penalty\ $C$inducesapath(resp.\ dipath)\penalty\ $\mathopen{\upharpoonleft}f\mathclose{\upharpoonright}$in$\mathopen{\upharpoonleft}C\mathclose{\upharpoonright}$.Itiseasytoshowthatgiventwodipaths$f,g:x\twoheadrightarrow y$in\penalty\ $C$,iftheyarehomotopic(resp.\ dihomotopic)thenthedipaths$\mathopen{\upharpoonleft}f\mathclose{\upharpoonright}$and$\mathopen{\upharpoonleft}g\mathclose{\upharpoonright}$arealsohomotopic(resp.\ dihomotopic).AconversepropertywasshownbyFajstrupforlocallyfiniteprecubicalsets\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{fajstrup2005dipaths}{}{}]}(moreexactly,thisresultwasformulatedforgeometricrealizationofprecubicalsetsasd-spaces,buttheproofadaptsstraightforwardlytothecaseofgeneralizedmetricspaces).\par\begin{prop}Given $C$ geometric and locally finite, a geometric and locally finite
precubical set, the homotopy (resp.\ dihomotopy) classes of dipaths from\penalty\ $x$
to\penalty\ $y$ in\penalty\ $C$ are in bijection with homotopy (resp.\ dihomotopy) classes of
dipaths from $\mathopen{\upharpoonleft}x\mathclose{\upharpoonright}$ to $\mathopen{\upharpoonleft}y\mathclose{\upharpoonright}$ in\penalty\ $\mathopen{\upharpoonleft}C\mathclose{\upharpoonright}$.
\end{prop}\par\par FromthepreviouspropositionandTheorem\penalty\ \ref{thm:hom-dihom},onecandeducethat,fordipathsbetweenanytwopointswhicharerealizationsofverticesof\penalty\ $C$,homotopycoincideswithdihomotopyin\penalty\ $\mathopen{\upharpoonleft}C\mathclose{\upharpoonright}$.Variantsofthispropertieshavebeenshownintheliterature:inthecaseofgeometricsemanticsof``simple^{\prime\prime}programswithmutexes\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{datc}{}{}]},inthecaseof\textsc{npc}cubecomplexes\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Ghrist2}{}{}]}(seealsonextsection).AsimilarobservationhasbeenmadeforspaceswhicharehypercontinuouslatticesequippedwiththeirLawsontopology,suchthattheirunderlyingspacehasconnectedCWtype\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{criterion}{}{}]}.Inthatcasealso,increasingpathsthatarehomotopicareinfactdihomotopic.\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Non-positively curved spaces}
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{CAT(0) spaces}
We now recall the notion of non-positively curved space (also called CAT(0)
space), with the aim of showing that this notion is a topological counterpart of
the notion introduced for precubical sets (Definition\penalty\ \ref{def:pcs-npc}). We
chose not to define this earlier in the paper in order to emphasize that this is
only an inspiration for our axiomatics on precubical sets, but the geometric
interpretation is not needed in order to work with non-positively curved
precubical sets (excepting, maybe, to forge intuitions).
\par This notion generalizes hyperbolic geometry by formalizing the observation that,
in a non-positively curved space, triangles with geodesic sides appear to be
thinner than in usual, flat, spaces. Its importance is due to the numerous
applications this notion has allowed. We only mention basic notions here, and
refer the reader to standard texts for a more detailed presentation of the
subject\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{gromov1987hyperbolic, bridson2009metric, ghys1990groupes}{}{}]}.
In the rest of this section, for simplicity, we only consider symmetric
generalized metric spaces, since the topology of a space does not depend on its
direction. Interesting possible generalizations in the directed case are
mentioned in Section\penalty\ \ref{sec:conclusion}.
\par\defi A \emph{geodesic triangle} $\Delta(x,y,z)$ in a geodesic metric space\penalty\ $X$
consists of three points $x$, $y$, $z$ and three geodesics joining any pair of
two. A \emph{comparison triangle} for a geodesic triangle $\Delta(x,y,z)$
consists of an isometry $\overline{-}:\Delta(x,y,z)\to\mathbb{R}^{2}$ whose image is a geodesic
triangle $\overline{\Delta}(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z})$, where\penalty\ $\mathbb{R}^{2}$ is equipped with the
usual euclidian distance $d_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}$.
\par\noindent We now recall the definition of non-positively curved space based on the
comparison axiom of Cartan, Alexandrof and Topogonov. This is the origin of the
name CAT(0), where the 0 refers to the fact that strictly positive or negative
curvature can also be defined in a similar way, but we will refrain from
introducing those in full generality here (see below).
\par\defi A geodesic triangle $\Delta(x,y,z)$ is \emph{CAT(0)} when there exists a
comparison triangle $\overline{\Delta}(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z})$ such that for any two
points $p,q\in\Delta(x,y,z)$, we have $d(p,q)\leqslant d_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\overline{p},\overline{q})$. A
geodesic metric space is \emph{CAT(0)} when every geodesic triangle is CAT(0),
and \emph{locally CAT(0)} or \emph{non-positively curved} ({npc}) when every
point admits a neighborhood which is CAT(0).
\par\noindent These spaces enjoy many nice properties such as being (locally) uniquely geodesic
and contractible. We refer the reader to\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bridson2009metric}{}{}]} for more details about those.
\par\par Other notions of curvature can be defined if we consider other ``model spaces''
instead of\penalty\ $\mathbb{R}^{2}$ in which we take our comparison triangles. This amounts, in
Definitions\penalty\ \ref{def:geodesic-triangle} and\penalty\ \ref{def:cat0}, to consider geodesic
triangles $\overline{-}:\Delta(x,y,z)\to M_{k}$ where $M_{k}$ is called a model space. For
$k=0$, we have $M_{k}=\mathbb{R}^{2}$ and we recover the above definitions; for $k>0$, $M_{k}$
is a 2-\hskip 0.0pt{}sphere (the smaller $k$ is, the bigger its radius is); for $k>0$,
$M_{k}$ is a hyperbolic space. Compared to usual model spaces $M_{0}$, triangles in
model spaces $M_{k}$ are fatter when $k>0$ and slimmer when $k<0$, this getting
more accentuated when $|k|$ becomes large.
In particular, for $k=1$, we consider the standard 2-\hskip 0.0pt{}sphere\penalty\ $\mathbb{S}^{2}$
equipped with the distance\penalty\ $d$ such that $d(x,y)\in[0,\pi]$ is defined by
$\cos(d(x,y))=\langle x,y\rangle$. A space is CAT(1) when for every
triangle\penalty\ $\Delta(x,y,z)$ whose diameter is less than $2\pi$, there exists a
comparison triangle $\overline{\Delta}(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z})$ in $\mathbb{S}^{2}$, such that we
have $d(p,q)\leqslant d_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\overline{p},\overline{q})$ for every points
$p,q\in\Delta(x,y,z)$.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{The Gromov link condition}
\par In this section we recall the characterization given by
Gromov\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{gromov1987hyperbolic}{}{}]} of CAT(0) cubical complexes, see
also\penalty\ \hbox{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bridson2009metric}{}{}, II.5.4]}} and\penalty\ \hbox{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bridson2009metric}{}{}, II.5.20]}}. We first introduce some
necessary definitions.
\par\defi[\hbox{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bridson2009metric}{}{}, Section 7.14]}}]
Given a geodesic metric space\penalty\ $X$ and a point $x\in X$, the \emph{link}
$\operatorname{link}_{X}(x)$ of $x$ in $X$ is the set of geodesics $\gamma:[0,a]\to X$ such
that $\gamma(0)=x$, equipped with the compact-open topology, quotiented by the
relation identifying two paths which coincide on an interval of the for
$[0,\varepsilon[$ for some $\varepsilon>0$, i.e.\ the ``directions'' from\penalty\ $x$
pointing into\penalty\ $X$. This space can be metrized using the angle between two such
directions.
\par\noindent This notion of link can be formally related to the one introduced for precubical
sets in Definition\penalty\ \ref{def:pcs-link} by observing that the former is a
geometric realization of the latter (where the standard $n$-simplex is realized
as a spherical simplex with edges of length $\pi/2$).
\par\par\defi An abstract simplicial complex is \emph{flag} if whenever the complex contains
the 1-skeleton of a simplex, it also contains the simplex: given vertices
$x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}$ such that $\left\{x_{i},x_{j}\right\}$ are simplices for every indices
$i,j$, the set $\left\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\right\}$ is also a simplex.
\par\noindent Again, this notion corresponds to the one of Definition\penalty\ \ref{def:flag} through
the geometric realization.
\par\par\par\par Finally, we can explain in which way our conditions for non-positively curved
precubical sets (Definition\penalty\ \ref{def:pcs-npc}) correspond to the traditional
geometric one.
\par\begin{thm}Given a finite dimensional geometric precubical set\penalty\ $C$, the following are
equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@[(i)]$\left|C\right|$ is non-positively curved
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@[(ii)]in $\left|C\right|$ the link of every point is CAT(1)
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@[(iii)]in $\left|C\right|$ the link of every vertex is a flag complex
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@[(iv)]in $C$ the link of every vertex is a flag complex
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@[(v)]$C$ satisfies the cube condition
\end{enumerate}Moreover, the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@[(i')]$\left|C\right|$ is CAT(0)
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@[(ii')]$\left|C\right|$ is uniquely geodesic
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@[(iii')]$\left|C\right|$ is non-positively curved and simply connected
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@[(iv')]$C$ satisfies the cube condition and is simply connected
\end{enumerate}\end{thm}\begin{proof}First, notice that because\penalty\ $C$ is supposed to be finite dimensional, its
realization is geodesic by Proposition\penalty\ \ref{prop:fd-pcs}. The equivalence
between (i), (ii) and (iii) is due to Gromov\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{gromov1987hyperbolic}{}{}]}, see
also\penalty\ \hbox{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bridson2009metric}{}{}, Thm. 5.20]}}. The equivalence between (iii) and (iv) is
immediate and the equivalence between (iv) and (v) was shown in
Theorem\penalty\ \ref{thm:pcs-npc-flag}.
The equivalence between (i'), (ii') and (iii') is due to
Gromov\penalty\ \hbox{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bridson2009metric}{}{}, Thm.\penalty\ 5.5]}}. The equivalence between (iii') and (iv')
follows from the equivalence between (i) and (v).
\end{proof}\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Conclusion and future work}
We showed in this article that numerous concepts from different fields of
mathematics and computer science are closely related, non-positively curved
spaces and rewriting systems with the cube property in particular, etc.{}
These are important observations since, in many ways, non-positively curved
spaces are ``simpler'' to work with than general spaces: topological invariants
are much simpler, e.g.\ the homology and homotopy groups have no torsion, and
their universal covering space are contractible \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bridson2009metric}{}{}]}.
\par There are many possible extensions to this work. First, in the case of a
non-positively curved precubical set, as used in the geometric semantics of
programs in Section\penalty\ \ref{sec:pcs}, we believe that the coreflexion of the
adjunction between (some form of labeled) precubical sets and (labeled) prime
event structures of\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{goubault2012formal}{}{}]} is directly linked to the
universal dicovering in the sense of\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{datc}{}{}]}. This would enlighten the
relationship between these two models, in case we are dealing with concurrent
programs with mutual exclusion primitives.
\par Our original idea was that for non-positively curved spaces\penalty\ $X$, dihomotopy of
dipaths can be characterized by the first relative homology group $H_{1}(X,A)$
where\penalty\ $A$ is the subspace of\penalty\ $X$ formed of the initial and final points of the
dipaths we are considering. By the work of Steiner\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{omegacatchaincomplex}{}{}]},
we know that the homotopy 2-categories, for some particular loop-free spaces,
are in one-to-one correspondence with some particular augmented directed
complexes, and that homotopic dipaths would map onto homologous elements of the
corresponding chain complex. In the case of non-positively curved spaces, this
implies that dihomotopic dipaths would be mapped onto homologous elements of the
underlying chain complex, in a not too distant manner, by Hurewicz theorem, i.e.\ up to abelianization. Unfortunately, it seems that we need to further shrink
down the class of spaces so that dihomotopy of dipaths can be fully
characterized by this first relative homology group, and there are probably
subtle combinatorial properties to be discovered.
\par We also expect to extend the results of this article to programs with resources
of arbitrary capacity, where we still have some control of the homotopy type of
the spaces generated by the semantics of such programs. This is, after all, part
of an endeavor to better understand the geometries that can appear in the study
of rewriting, concurrent and distributed systems, where other primitives
(test-and-set and all read-modify-write statements) will generate different
types of geometries. The relationship between metrics and (di-)homotopies,
unravelled in this paper, may also pave the way to considering timed concurrent
models, studied up to deformation (and amenable to state-space reduction
techniques\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{datc}{}{}]}). This has actually already been used in a different
context, in robotics, by Ghrist and collaborators, to design efficient
algorithms, see e.g.\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Ghrist2}{}{}]}.
